Right Now Is the Perfect Time to Play the Quietly Best Yakuza Spinoff

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

When Like a Dragon Gaiden: The Man Who Erased His Name was released in 2023, the Yakuza series was in a strange position. The team at Sega's Ryu Ga Gotoku Studio was gearing up to give a heroic sendoff to longtime protagonist Kazuma Kiryu. They had big plans for 2024's Like a Dragon: Infinite Wealth to pass the torch to a new main character. But Yakuza had unexpectedly become bigger than ever outside Japan, thanks largely to the viral success of Yakuza 0 throughout the late 2010s and early 2020s. The fans demanded more Kiryu, not less. And developer Ryu Ga Gotoku Studio rose to the occasion with The Man Who Erased His Name, an interquel that depicts his lonely years in exile after the events of Yakuza 6.

"We could have just told of Kiryu’s past through a thirty-minute interlude as part of Like a Dragon 8 [Infinite Wealth], but we decided it would be a lot more interesting as a game of its own, which is how the project came to be," series producer Masayoshi Yokoyama told Automaton in October 2023. "Though this meant having a whole extra game to make, it still uses the same engine, so we thought 'It’s not like we have to make it from scratch,' and decided to go with it. In the end, it took us about half a year to make it."

While this doesn't sound all that promising, The Man Who Erased His Name delivers some of the series' all-time best storytelling and character work. With Japan's criminal underworld forever changed after a decades-long government conspiracy comes to light inYakuza 6, Kiryu must work with the Daidoji Clan, a shadowy organization of spies and fixers. Our hero knows a bit too much about the connections between the government, police, and organized crime, so the Daidoji threaten his family to ensure he plays along. Armed with identity-concealing sunglasses and magic cigarettes, Kiryu assumes the identity of the secret agent Joryu after faking his own death.

While he's on the clock, Joryu's dishing out pain to dozens of goons at a time. When he's not, Kiryu is adrift and miserable, isolated from everyone he's ever cared about. Yakuza players will be accustomed to seeing Kiryu with the odds stacked against him, but this is a gloomy rock bottom like never before. He spends much of his time meditating in a buddhist temple or smoking countless cigarettes. It's probably best enjoyed if you've played at least one other game in the series, but if you manage to make it through the ending of The Man Who Erased His Name without shedding a tear, you're made of solid stone.

Compared to the mainline Yakuza games, The Man Who Erased His Name is a brief, compact experience — just 15-20 hours compared to the usual 60+. You'll spend most of your time in the familiar Sotenbori district of Osaka, a staple location of the series, as well as the Isezaki Ijincho district of Yokohama from Like a Dragon and Infinite Wealth. The sidequel also adds a new location, the floating vice ship known as the Castle, which features a fighting arena, a cabaret club, Pocket Circuit racing, and the inevitable karaoke.

Best of all, The Man Who Erased His Name is significantly lowered-price on the PlayStation Store right now, discounted from $49.99 to $9.99. The sale runs through Nov. 21. The game is also available on Windows PC and Xbox Series X.

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

’Obsession’s Breakout Star Is Officially Breaking Records Again

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

There are few names that had a bigger breakout year in 2026 than Inde Navarrette. From being relatively unknown last year to one of the hottest names in Hollywood right now, Navarrette's rise to the top has been quick and justified. Her success can be chalked up to a starring turn in the year's most viral film, where she stunned as Nikki Freeman in Focus Features' Obsession.

Called "one of the year's best" performances by Hannah Hunt in her review for Collider, some are even calling for Navarrette to become an Oscar winner for the role, although she remains a firm second favorite behind Julianne Moore for her lead role in Jesse Eisenberg's The Debut. However, now that she is set to portray Rogue in the MCU's X-Men reboot, whether she wins the Academy Award won't change how big a presence she will have on our screens for years to come. But Navarrette's success so far isn't limited to Obsession.

After helping the horror breakout achieve multiple records at the box office, Navarrette has shifted her stardom to one of America's oldest and most beloved institutions and made waves there too. It's been reported that Navarrette's Halloween episode of Saturday Night Live earned 5.2 million viewers, the biggest of the season so far. In fact, these figures rank high when compared to previous seasons, with only a handful of episodes outperforming it over recent years. Gracie Abrams joined Navarrette as the musical guest on her season-high episode, with the next challenge for the throne being the pairing of Druski and The Strokes, who will take to the stage this Saturday.

Navarrette is making a habit of setting trends and breaking records. During its well-documented box office run in which it toppled the latest Star Wars movie, Obsession became the highest-grossing non-animated original film of the past decade, the highest-grossing original horror movie of this century, and the fifth highest-grossing horror film of all time. Outside the holiday corridor, it also became the first film since E.T. to grow in ticket sales across three consecutive weekends, and did all this on a budget of less than $1 million.

You'll be able to stream Obsession on Netflix on November 17. For more of the latest stories, make sure to stay tuned to Collider.

New Bromley boss Karl Robinson heaps praise on 'megastar' Jermain Defoe - with Premier League legend in talks over dugout return after walking out on Woking

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published: 08:00 EDT, 6 November 2026 | Updated: 08:39 EDT, 6 November 2026

New Bromley boss Karl Robinson has hailed Premier League 'megastar' Jermain Defoe as the ultimate professional following his abrupt departure from Woking.

Daily Mail Sport revealed on Tuesday that Defoe is in talks to join Robinson's backroom staff at League Two strugglers Bromley after Andy Woodman's dismissal last month.

And while his appointment has yet to be confirmed, Robinson jumped at the chance to wax lyrical about Defoe at his inauguration on Thursday.

Bromley sit bottom of League Two but their new boss believes Defoe's meticulous attention to detail would help the players find another gear.

'There's been a lot of talk but there always is with names like Jermain Defoe, who is a megastar. He's one of the greatest Premier League strikers of all time,' Robinson said.

'I have to raise and change the narrative of what this stands for,' the new boss pointed to the Bromley badge on his jumper.

Jermain Defoe is in talks to join League One strugglers Bromley as Karl Robinson's assistant

Former Salford City manager Robinson has hailed 'megastar' Defoe as the ultimate professional

'I have to bring people in to make me the best version of myself. I spoke to people who know Jermain and played with him.

'He was always first in. All the players who have played with him will tell you that. Steven Gerrard will tell you that.

'He was first in and still in the training ground at 4pm - massages, recovery, gym, video analysis of the players he's playing against.

'I was speaking to him about something completely different the other day and we were talking about discipline and professionalism.

'I said to him: "what do you do that for?". He went: "If I do everything right Monday to Friday and I play poorly, I can accept it because I've done everything in the week to be the best version of myself."

'I'm listening and I've got goose pimples on my arm. Then I'm thinking, that's probably why you're Jermain Defoe, because he did everything he possibly could to maximise his talent.

Defoe has been out of work since walking away from National League side Woking after just four months at the helm amid a strained relationship with the club's director of football.

The 44-year-old took charge of the final six games of last season and was expected to lead the club into the new campaign.

However, he failed to report for work on the eve of the season before contacting the club later that day to inform them he would not be returning.

The former England international and Tottenham Hotspur striker would add top-level experience to the dressing room, having scored 163 Premier League goals in 496 appearances - ranking 10th on the Premier League's scoring charts.

Robinson spoke as though he idolised Defoe when quizzed on his potential appointment, highlighting his humility and relentlessness as key assets to succeed as a coach.

'Failure isn't falling short, it's when you don't maximise your talent. And I think some of the players here haven't maximised their athletic, technical and tactical capabilities', Robinson added.

'For whatever reason, we need to find the key to what that is. I think someone like Jermain (would bring that) because he maximised everything he was and everything he is.

'That's almost the secret behind the genius of the top players. It's their attention to detail, professionalism and discipline – he does that over and over again.

'He's tried to manage in non-league as well. That, in itself, is dropping your ego to learn.'

Although Bromley are still working on finalising Defoe's appointment, he is expected to join Robinson in the dugout at Hayes Lane. The Ravens host Rotherham in the first round of the FA Cup this weekend as Robinson takes his first game in charge.

Flattering and Low-Maintenance, the Midi Bob With Bangs Is the Simple Way to Update Your Hairstyle

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It’s the noncommittal way to embrace shorter lengths.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Short haircuts such as a pixie crop or a jaw-skimming bob never fail to make an impact, creating a beauty mood that feels instantly fresh while bringing out the very best of your natural hair texture.

But if you’re not ready to take the plunge or fancy a more gradual change, take a leaf out of Taylor Swift’s book with a “midi” bob and a fringe to add structure and interest. Swift spent a majority of the last few years with long hair, so getting incremental trims is not only a great way to remove any dry or split ends, but it also helps ease you into a shorter haircut.

“The midi bob sits in that sweet spot between a classic bob and longer hair. It usually falls somewhere between the jawline and collarbone, which gives it a softer, more lived-in feel than a sharper, shorter cut,” says Luke Hersheson, hairstylist and CEO of Hershesons. “What I like about it is that it doesn’t feel too ‘done’ or too precious. Adding a fringe instantly brings another dimension to the shape. It frames the face, breaks up the length, and gives the cut more personality. It’s often that little detail that takes a simple bob and makes it feel individual.”

Hairstylist and founder of Arkive Headcare, Adam Reed, agrees, adding: “It’s the balance between something beautifully classic and something that feels incredibly modern. The bob is one of those haircuts that continually reinvents itself, and I think the midi length is particularly appealing because it doesn’t feel too restrictive.”

The best news about a midi bob with fringe is also the versatility. You can still have it up or down, and it can be easily tailored to every hair type and curl pattern. “The fringe can be adapted too,” adds Hersheson. “Whether that’s a curtain fringe, a softer bottleneck shape, or something fuller, it’s really about finding the version that works with your natural texture rather than fighting against it.”

As with all haircuts, the main consideration should be to understand how your hair naturally behaves. “A fringe sits front and center, so things like cowlicks, growth patterns, and texture matter,” says Hersheson.

He adds: “You also need to think about the relationship between the fringe and the rest of the cut. A bob can sometimes feel quite graphic, so I often prefer a fringe that has a bit of softness and movement through it. It keeps the overall look modern and wearable.”

If you’re new to fringes, try a longer curtain fringe or a soft side-swept style as they are more versatile, shares Reed. Fringes do require some maintenance and regular trimming, so tell your hairdresser to tailor it to your lifestyle. “My biggest piece of advice is to bring references, but allow your hairdresser to interpret them for your hair rather than trying to recreate somebody else’s fringe exactly,” Reed tells ELLE.

There is no one-size-fits-all when it comes to hair, but Hersheson shares his main tips to ensure a polished yet relaxed finish, no matter your hair type.

Curly hair: Don’t try to force curls into a bob shape they don’t want to be. Work with the natural movement and focus on creating a strong silhouette. The Almost Everything Cream and a bit of diffused drying will usually give you a much better result than over-styling. Fringes on curly hair can look brilliant when they’re slightly looser and less perfect.

Thick hair: It’s all about using the weight of your hair to your advantage. Internal layers can stop the shape from becoming triangular while still keeping that fullness people love. When styling, don’t over-smooth it. A little texture is what makes a midi bob feel modern.

Straight hair: This hair type spotlights the silhouette of a sharper bob, but if your hair is prone to looking flat, add texture through the ends or use a texture spray to take away that freshly styled finish. The goal is for it to look like you’ve lived in it for a day or two.

Fine hair: A midi bob can actually make fine hair appear much thicker because you’re concentrating the weight into a stronger shape. Keep products light and avoid anything too heavy around the roots. A soft fringe can create the illusion of fullness around the front and make the entire haircut feel more substantial.

Scientists Found the Secret to Real-Life Invisible Tech—and It’s Amazingly Simple

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Northwestern engineers used AI to sift 20,000 designs for a drone built around a flaw in human sight.

Invisibility is the sci-fi dream. Whether a Klingon Bird-of-Prey or James Bond’s Aston Martin, it’s undeniably cool for an object to blink out of existence—or at least, as it appears to our naked eye. However, while impressive stealth technologies can render aircraft invisible to electromagnetic “eyesight” such as radar (and some have even tried to channel Q’s engineering genius), tricking the human eye has proved to be much more difficult.

That’s why engineers at Northwestern University tried a different approach when attempting to design an “invisible” drone. Instead of using a complicated system of cameras, camouflage, or other light-bending materials, they went with another, more simple tactic: Just spin the thing really fast. The result was the Phantom Twist, a palm-sized drone capable of spinning 25 times per second. At that speed, the human eye can't keep up, and the drone shows up as little more than a faint blur. Details of this new device, which weighs little more than an ounce, were presented at the Robotics: Science and Systems 2026 conference in Sydney, Australia earlier this summer.

“Most efforts to hide drones focus on making them look like their surroundings,” Northwestern’s Michael Rubenstein, who led the work, said in a press statement. “Instead, we asked whether we could design the drone itself around the way humans perceive motion. This idea of low visibility through persistent motion is something few people have explored.”

One of those “few” was Michael Dammar. Back in 2006, the inventor was working for Minneapolis-based VeraTech, which patented a y-shaped, boomerang-like drone that could spin fast enough to become invisible to a casual observer. At the time, the company saw possibilities for the drone in the realm of military surveillance, and a few years later, engineers at MIT developed a different kind of monocopter, though their version was very much visible.

To build the Phantom Twist, the team used AI-driven modeling to generate up to 20,000 drone designs that could fly. Optimization algorithms then rearranged the drone’s main parts (a motor, propeller, circuit board, counterweight, and batteries) to find the best layout for stable flight and, yes, invisibility. The result is an extremely small machine that looks just like a small blur against the background as it rapidly speeds around its center of gravity. But the drone isn’t actually invisible. Its disappearing act comes from a limitation in how our eyes process motion, not from anything that hides the drone itself.

“The human eye takes time to accumulate signals, roughly analogous to the exposure time of a camera,” Northwestern’s Emma Alexander, who was also involved in the project, said in a press statement. “When an object spins quickly, we perceive it as blurring out and losing distinct features. Because this new drone is almost entirely transparent, its few opaque components are visually averaged with the background for an overall appearance of a slight haze.”

The researchers hope the tiny drone will be a boon for inspecting infrastructure without visual distraction of passing drivers, for example, and also a less-invasive method for tracking wildlife. Although for the drone to truly help biologists keep tabs on elusive species, engineers will need to perfect one of the drone’s core weaknesses—despite its small size, the drone is mighty noisy.

Rubenstein hopes that future iterations of the drone will feature more transparent support structures and quieter propulsion so that the Phantom Twist truly lives up to its ghostly name.

Darren lives in Portland, has a cat, and writes/edits about sci-fi and how our world works. You can find his previous stuff at Gizmodo and Paste if you look hard enough.

Honda’s HRC Performance Parts Business Was a Long Time Coming

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Oh, and the Civic Type R HRC concept? Yeah, it’s gonna be sold here in the United States.

Honda has made no secret of its plans to eventually enter the performance parts business with HRC-branded components. This is part of a larger campaign to turn the HRC brand—now the global name for all Honda motorsports activities—into a performance footprint on par with Nissan’s Nismo or BMW’s M. And like those brands, it sure seems like complete production models will be sold with that performance badge, too.

Both HRC performance parts and HRC road cars were a hot topic at the 2026 SEMA Show, where the company showed off race cars, custom builds, and one very interesting concept. In an interview with Road & Track, HRC U.S. senior vice president Jon Ikeda made it clear that the Civic Type R HRC concept on display (seen below) was “going to be sold here.”

“We’ve shared with everyone that it’s coming here,” Ikeda added, “and we’re really excited about it. We have big debuts coming next year—at the Tokyo Auto Salon, a big debut there. We’re just happy that our region is included; there are a lot of people who are excited about it here.”

While he stopped short of confirming similar plans for an Acura Integra Type S HRC, Ikeda noted that consumer interest is strong and suggested that the company “[has] to seriously think about doing it.”

An HRC Integra may not be a lock, but Honda has already confirmed that the four-door Acura will join the Civic Type R in receiving performance parts offered directly by HRC. Ikeda notes that the Integra’s offerings will include wheels from 2Elle, the supplier of the forged wheels used on the recently-retired ARX-06 race car. Those, and other components, were tested on an Integra Type S at the Nürburgring Nordschleife ahead of the SEMA showcase. Ikeda mentions the value of these parts being sold directly by the OEM—a major distinction that keeps options open for owners worried about voiding a warranty or decreasing a car’s resale value.

“As soon as you accessorize,” he continues, “you void your warranty on things. If you own a Honda car, you do a third party purchase of a spring or something, you devalue your vehicle. By creating OEM parts, you’re able to honor the warranty when you buy the car, you can even finance it, bundle it up in your financing if you want if you do it at the dealership. If you have all the OEM parts to upgrade, it’s not a new business framework. Others are doing it. We have a very equitable brand to say, ‘hey, you’ve got OEM HRC parts, put it on your car, get your warranty, don’t devalue your vehicle, enjoy the ride.’ That’s a big, big part of it.”

HRC parts will begin arriving at dealers in the first quarter of next year. The HRC-badged Civic Type R should be revealed at the Tokyo Auto Salon in January.

Tested: 2027 Mercedes-AMG GT 55 4-Door Proves EVs Can Be Engaging

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This is an electric car that makes V-8 noises, goes like stink, and is loaded to the gills with Mercedes luxury.

Making a performance EV compelling enough to convince enthusiasts to jump on board has proven difficult, but that’s precisely what Mercedes-AMG is trying to do with the 2027 4-Door Coupe. Mercedes is going down the same path as other manufacturers and their acclaimed electric performance machines—such as the Hyundai Ioniq 5 N and Genesis GV60 Magma—by simulating the experience of a car equipped with an internal-combustion engine. It’s great fun, but the efforts would be for naught if the rest of the car didn’t live up to the experience. Thankfully, this electric AMG is a ripper from every angle. Except when viewed from the rear. Maybe avoid doing that.

Our expert editors test every vehicle we review. Read more about how we test and review cars here.

It’s hardly lesser, but the “55” in the AMG GT 55 4-Door Coupe is empirically less mighty than the “63” in the GT 63. This EV features a tri-motor powertrain made up of three axial-flux motors engineered with YASA that combine for 805 hp and 1372 pound-feet of torque. That 805 hp figure is the peak, the lesser “continuous” rating coming in at 557 hp. But if you want all the power, all the time, that can be accomplished by putting the car in Race mode, or pulling both steering-wheel paddles to activate Boost mode, which lasts for 55 seconds. Mercedes claims a 2.7-second 0–60-mph time and top speed of 186 mph should you option the AMG Performance package.

Powertrain layout: 2 rear electric motors, 1 front

Slipping behind the wheel, the first thing that struck me about the GT 55 was the properly low seating position. Excellent work there, Mercedes. After fumbling about with the confusing start/stop button on the stalk (the car doesn’t really turn on until you flick it into Drive or Reverse), I started comfortably trundling along.

The AMG Active Ride Control air suspension did a standup job on the track in the GT 63, but it’s even more remarkable on the road in the 55. An air suspension with adaptive dampers is standard, but this optional hydraulically interconnected damper system is assuredly the one you want. Combined with the multilink suspension in the front and rear, an abundance of forged aluminum components on the front axle, and rear-axle steering up to six degrees, this chassis just oozes confidence right off the bat. There was no jittery stiffness or harshness to the ride in Comfort, and it was only moderately sharper on bumps in the punchier modes. You could spend every day behind the wheel thinking you’re in a regular Mercedes and not an AMG product.

But when I found some sweepers, the front end just begged for me to keep piling on speed thanks to how stable everything feels. At points, it was almost too confidence-inspiring, as the sophisticated chassis was writing checks the GT’s 5501-pound mass and Continental SportContact 7 summer tires struggled to cash on some of southern Spain’s steep downhill slaloms. The steering and body control say go, go, but the tires quickly say they need a break from the heat. Don’t mistake that as a braking system limitation either, because the optional carbon-ceramics hold up to mega abuse. I just wish the pedal feel wasn’t so mushy and inconsistent throughout the stroke.

Accelerating out of corners, the sensation was AMG at its best. With the ESP system in Sport mode, the rear tires surrendered grip progressively and tidily. It was so consistent and predictable that it almost felt calculated, and to a degree, it is. Mercedes let me play with the AMG Race Engineer system in the 63 model on the racetrack—that’s the three dials in the center console which let the driver customize how hairy or locked down they want the experience from a traction control and yaw agility perspective. The 55 has the exact same system, given you option it. It’s fun to toy with on the track but can just as easily change the character of the car on a fast canyon road to suit your mood.

And then there’s AMGForce Sport+, the system that simulates both a V-8 engine and a multi-clutch transmission system and that proved remarkably adept at convincing me I was piloting an internal-combustion-powered car. Mercedes mimics the soundtrack and other theatrics—engine braking, seat vibrations, and shifting—from the previous-gen AMG GT R Coupe. I found myself tapping up and down the paddles through the mountains with glee for miles on end. Yeah, it’s all fake, but the alternative is a pure electric car experience that I find gets a bit boring after a short period.

Would a proper V-8 and real transmission have been more involving and addicting to wind out? Absolutely. But even if you set aside the stagecraft, this AMG is a blast to drive. It toes the line between back-road barn burner and everyday luxury like the very best AMGs of recent history. Plus, the EV hardware backing everything up is elite.

A 106-kWh battery pack developed specifically for AMG performance cars can be charged at speeds over 600 kW, allowing you to go from 10 to 80 percent in only 11 minutes. No, 600-kW chargers don’t exist in the U.S. yet, but when they arrive, this car will be ready. The system can even switch from 800 to 400 volts by virtually “splitting” the pack in half for less compromised charging at 400-volt stations, which should help tremendously at Tesla Superchargers. Range figures aren’t out yet, but Mercedes is claiming up to 434 miles on the optimistic European WLTP test, so you can rest assured that EPA numbers will be well over 300 miles.

Mercedes has clearly done some learning from all the criticism leveled at its interiors over the past half-decade. The GT’s steering wheel has a proper scroll wheel for volume and a switch for cruise control. The door-positioned seat toggles physically move when you press them (finally), and those AMG Race Engineer controls in the center console are aptly the most satisfying knobs to turn in the whole car. The quilted leather on the doors themselves is spectacular, but don’t worry, there’s plenty to wrinkle a brow at too.

Adjusting the heated and ventilated seat controls requires multiple extremely precise stabs at the touchscreen. Trying to find your way around the unending drive mode and sound mode controls will require patience for even the most tech-savvy. And while the massive screens are of superb quality in terms of both picture and response, the sheer amount of real estate dedicated to them could easily be a turn-off. On the plus side, the dedicated tachometer that pops up in Sport+ mode is probably the best digital tach fitted to a Mercedes yet. Ironic that it arrives in an EV.

Practicality doesn’t take a back seat with this long, swoopy liftback. Despite its name, this is a four-door hatchback, and unlike the Porsche Taycan, it has enough space in the rear to seat medium-to-tall adults. It doesn’t completely suck to get in or out either. In another win for the AMG over Porsche’s sport sedan, the 14.7-cubic-foot cargo area seems positively massive. You can extend it further by putting the rear seats down, leaving an impressively large space to go road-tripping or move large items with. A carbon-fiber frunk exists for smaller items, too, and it’s here you’ll find the traditional signed AMG plaque, only this one is signed by any number of folks involved in the design or engineering of the vehicle.

If you found the Taycan a little too serious or not practical enough, the AMG GT 55 4-Door Coupe might be just the EV you’re looking for. It lands in between the Taycan 4S and GTS, but is quicker than both of those Taycan variants in a straight line. The Porsche is the more serious sports car, though practicality is firmly on Mercedes’s side. The debate between the 63 and 55 is where things get a little tricky with about a $30,000 gulf between the two—the 55 starts at $146,500, the 63 at $176,550.

Then there’s the forthcoming fully electric BMW M3 to consider, but we need to wait for pricing on that. But putting that and the GT together already feels as if it will be one of the benchmark tests of 2027.

I didn’t drive the GT 63 on the road, but it didn’t feel significantly quicker than the GT 55 in a straight line. Both have the same overengineered battery pack and tri-motor powertrain. The differences are largely in the suspension and software, like the AMG Race Engineer (again, optional on the 55) and pure luxury features like massage seats and an enormous HUD. That said, when you’re spending $145K and some change for options, what’s another $30,000 to get it all? If extra-dynamic driving isn’t in your future, perhaps a base 55 without all the go-fast goodies makes fiscal sense. But otherwise, it’s hard to imagine anyone who has sold themselves on the idea of the GT 4-Door not wanting the full 63 experience.

The active rear diffuser takes moving aero to a new level of complex and cool. Mercedes says it provides a boost to efficiency on the highway, but it also helps stability at high speeds. Watching it extend and retract on the move is quite the party trick. And thankfully, you can manually extend it to show off to your friends who are stuck with passive rear diffusers on their cars.

Where's the growth? Legacy automakers face a tougher 2027 as costs rise, market tightens

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

DETROIT — There's a growing concern that the global automotive industry may be stuck in neutral, if not reverse, heading into 2027.

The auto industry — from the companies to their customers — has been surprisingly resilient this decade, but there are fears that rising costs, weaker vehicle pricing and a more challenging market could threaten profits and growth going forward.

Automakers also face pressure outside the U.S. from an onslaught of Chinese carmakers, a thinner new-product pipeline after retreating from some all-electric plans, and company- and region-specific challenges.

"With investor focus increasingly shifting to 2027, the setup appears challenging," Barclays analyst Dan Levy said in an investor note last month. "Rising inflation, continued China pressure, affordability concerns in the U.S., and a flat global production outlook suggest another year of limited earnings growth."

Several Wall Street analysts have said automakers will need continued pricing strength and additional cost savings to offset inflation, higher investment spending and a potentially weaker economy just to maintain their current levels.

"It's a little bit more optimistic in the U.S. and then very pessimistic in Europe," RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan told CNBC. "As a whole, there's some negativity on '27."

Automakers from the U.S. to Europe to Asia have been facing a growing list of headwinds.

For U.S.-based legacy automakers, Ford Motor said it expects next year's results to be in line with 2026, while General Motors grew more cautious about its ability to continue growing earnings as much as it had previously expected.

"We continue to believe that 2027 will be another strong year and better than 2026, assuming customer demand remains strong, and commodity, logistics and other recent cost pressures moderate," GM CEO Mary Barra told investors Oct. 20.

GM CFO Paul Jacobson said that while it's too early to officially forecast, the company is facing additional inflationary costs that could lead to another $2 billion headwind in 2027, like it expects this year. He specifically cited costs of commodity inflation, logistics and DRAM, or dynamic random-access memory chips as challenges.

Those chips are critical components in modern vehicles and are used for everything from temporary data storage and real-time processing from infotainment systems to safety-critical systems.

GM and others have been able to offset outside pressures through cost cuts, operational efficiencies and resilient consumer demand for higher-margin vehicles. However, there's a limit to what companies can do.

"There's only so much pricing you can push to consumers," Narayan said. "They're already at pretty high pricing."

European automakers including Volkswagen, Mercedes-Benz and BMW in recent weeks reduced their 2026 earnings forecasts in response to U.S. tariffs, the growth of Chinese automakers and rising costs.

Most recently, BMW this week said accelerated restructuring measures would reduce its automotive pretax earnings margin this year by 2.5 percentage points. The company said the restructuring should support "positive effects" into 2027 and a 3% to 5% pretax profit margin for 2028. That compares with a 5.3% automotive margin last year and 3.4% during the third quarter.

Chrysler parent Stellantis, which has large operations in Europe and the U.S., is facing its own challenges, as it's in the midst of a restructuring that aims for a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin this year. It has maintained a 2026 guidance that includes a mid-single-digit percentage increase in net revenue and a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin.

In Asia, South Korean automaker Hyundai Motor, which has experienced notable growth in recent years, also cut key metrics when it reported earnings late last month. The company lowered its 2026 wholesales target to 4 million vehicles, down from 4.16 million, and cut its operating-margin target to between 5.5% and 6.5%, down from a previous goal of 6.3% to 7.3%.

Toyota Motor is an outlier for its most recent quarter and outlook, as the world's largest automaker increased its full-year operating income forecast by 500 billion Japanese yen (about $3.2 billion) on Thursday.

Toyota's results were boosted by weakness in the Japanese yen as well as strong hybrid vehicle demand in the U.S. However, the increase in operating income was its first in six quarters.

Broadly, the changes to earnings and outlooks come as production for the global industry slows.

Consulting and insights firm GlobalData expects global light-vehicle production of about 90.96 million units in 2026, down 2.1% compared with last year. It forecast a production increase of less than 1% next year. That comes on the heels of strong annual growth earlier this decade, led by China.

Cox Automotive reported the average transaction price of a new U.S. vehicle in September was more than $50,500. That was just shy of the record of $50,614 in December 2025.

Lofty vehicle prices have led to record-high and record-long financing deals, as well as concerns that customers are getting priced out of the new-vehicle market.

The typical U.S. new-vehicle payment reached $776 a month in September, the highest since December 2023, according to Cox Automotive.

Cox Automotive chief economist Jeremy Robb, discussing the third quarter for the automotive industry, rated the overall economy, buyer ability and borrowing costs as cautionary "yellow lights."

But he did have some "green lights," such as good credit availability and employment conditions, which could give some relief for automakers and consumers, many of whom need vehicles for their everyday lives.

Robb said there's a "tug-of-war" between headwinds such as rising costs, interest rates and fuel prices and tailwinds such as growth in liquid assets and savings that has helped offset some of the additional costs.

"Consumers remain under financial pressure, but the vehicle demand continues to hold, showing more resiliency in the auto industry than we've seen in other parts of the economy," Robb said during a Sept. 24 call, noting consumer sentiment remains low.

Max Power: NASA hosting its 1st air show this weekend on Florida's Space Coast

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

You can learn about NASA tech and watch agency chief Jared Isaacman zoom overhead in a MiG jet.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

NASA will host a first-of-its-kind air show and aerospace expo on Florida's Space Coast this weekend.

The show, called Max Power, will take place Saturday and Sunday (Nov. 7 and Nov. 8) at and around NASA's Kennedy Space Center (KSC). That name should be familiar to "Simpsons" fans, by the way: Max Power is an alias that Homer uses in the 13th episode of the show's 10th season. (He got it off a hair dryer.)

There will be a lot to see at NASA's event, so you should consider dropping by if you're on the Space Coast this weekend. (Tickets are required; you can buy them here.) Here are a few of the highlights.

NASA will also hold a livestreamed press conference on Saturday at 5 p.m. EST (2200 GMT) about its moon base plans. It will feature Fox, García-Galán, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman and Dayna Ise, the acting director of the Utility and Infrastructure Office for the Moon Base Program.

There will also be dramatic flyovers on both days of Max Power, some of them by Isaacman, who's an accomplished pilot as well as a private astronaut. (He funded and commanded SpaceX's Inspiration4 and Polaris Dawn missions, which flew to Earth orbit in September 2021 and September 2024, respectively.)

According to NASA's Max Power schedule, Isaacman will perform flyovers both Saturday and Sunday at 11:58 a.m (in an F-5 jet) and 1:31 p.m. (in a MiG-29).

There will also be a lot to see as you're wandering the grounds at the Max Power sites.

The event "will showcase the newest aircraft, autonomous vehicles, technologies, and more defining the future of air and space," agency officials wrote in a Max Power description. "NASA will display its quiet supersonic X-59 plane, and agency partners will showcase moon rovers, spacecraft and more."

Michael Wall is the Spaceflight and Tech Editor for Space.com and joined the team in 2010. He primarily covers human and robotic spaceflight, military space, and exoplanets, but has been known to dabble in the space art beat. His book about the search for alien life, "Out There," was published on Nov. 13, 2018. Before becoming a science writer, Michael worked as a herpetologist and wildlife biologist. He has a Ph.D. in evolutionary biology from the University of Sydney, Australia, a bachelor's degree from the University of Arizona, and a graduate certificate in science writing from the University of California, Santa Cruz. To find out what his latest project is, you can follow Michael on Twitter.

Kevin Costner Is Particular About Scents—So He Made His Own Colognes

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Eight years ago, Kevin Costner was in Germany on a press tour. His hotel room was chock full of fruit baskets and other fancy accouterments that come with international fame. And also a handful of bath products. “I’m taking a shower and I’m thinking, ‘Man, this body wash smells good,’” he tells me from the couch of his modern white compound on a seaside street in Carpinteria, California. “And I’m thinking, ‘I don’t really use those words. Why would I even think that to myself?” Maybe two more times on that 15-day junket, he found himself thinking about the scent of that body wash.

“Can’t tell you why, don’t even know what it was,” he says. “I came back home to America, and probably over the next year, periodically, I actually thought about it. But then it faded in like a song you can’t remember.”

A few years later, he found himself in Scotland, scoring his Western epic Horizon. After a jetlagged detour to a countryside castle, he hopped in a hotel shower in Glasgow, and there it was: the smell. “I looked down and there were the bottles and I go, ‘That’s that smell from five or six years ago. That’s that smell,’" he recounts with his English lab, Bob, curled up at his side. “And I did something incredibly stupid. I went out of the shower and I got my phone.”

He got water everywhere in the process, but at that moment he was singularly focused. “I go back in the shower and I take a picture of these bottles as if they were going to disappear. I could have just really finished my shower, honestly. Dried off like a normal human being. But what I’m saying was it fired my senses again, imagination. And I sent those pictures back and I said, ‘See if you can get a case of this. I’m never going to forget it again.’”

It turned out to be 19-69’s Rainbow Bar, a scent inspired by both the ‘80s glam rock bands that frequented the Rainbow Bar on the Sunset Strip, along with general notes of California: there’s bourbon alongside basil and vetiver, creating a fragrance that’s simultaneously woody and green. That kickstarted Costner’s career as a perfumer. Now, he’s debuting a collection of fragrances with the same Milan-based niche perfume house. The collection consists of three fragrances (or extrait de parfum, to be precise), chicly advertised on 19-69’s website with a photo of Kevin Costner’s star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame. They retail at $80 per 7.5 milliliter bottle (a 30 ML bottle will run you $300, and the really giant 95 ML version costs a cool $800), and each fragrance—called After Hours, Heritage, and Pacific Coast Highway—reflects something of Costner’s life and history.

He tells me this story from his living room, amid coffee table books on design and the American frontier, and a homemade scrapbook dedicated to Bob’s first year of life (the dog peed a little bit on my foot, but I took it as a compliment). I visited on a foggy day in June, walking into his home to be greeted by a view of the Pacific. Costner, who has an endless string of massive, monocultural hits—Field of Dreams, The Bodyguard, the tv show Yellowstone—has the aura of a Paul Newman-type, his bright blue eyes undiminished by the trail of drool and dog hair that Bob leave on his feather-light black sweater. The peculiarity that we are talking about his collaboration with 19-69, and not an upcoming film of his, is not lost on him. “I bring just a lot of feeling to things because I can’t always explain why I like something,” he says. “It’s like wine. I think, ‘oh, it smells like wood.’ But does it, really? The fuck it does.”

After his second encounter with 19-69 products in Scotland, Costner’s assistant eventually tracked the brand’s co-founder Johan Bergelin down, and 19-69 sent him a case of the product. In 2024, he called Bergelin, to congratulate him on a job well done. They met in person for the first time that August in Cologne, Germany, which was not on purpose but did make them laugh. Shortly after that first meeting, they went ahead with a partnership, spending two years developing a collection of fragrances, produced in France.

As a child growing up in a small town in the Skåne region of Sweden, Bergelin had, much like Costner, been seduced by a scent he encountered in the wild. His was a perfume at the local hair salon, a mass market scent from Karl Lagerfeld. An artist and designer, he eventually became a self-taught perfumer, and launched 19-69 in 2017 with his wife, Catharina, who handles communications and writes the colorful copy for each scent. The Milan-based brand—initially inspired by the culture and societal upheaval of 1969, the year of Bergelin’s birth—is known amongst self-described “fragheads” for its offerings tied to specific eras and places (Christopher Street, the Rainbow Bar in Hollywood, the island of Capri): their Miami Blue scent has a note of cocaine; the American Psycho perfume, a collaboration with Bret Easton Ellis (which just won an Allure “Best of Beauty” award), contains hints of palate-cleansing sorbet and harkens back to the products in Patrick Bateman’s prophetic 1980s beauty routine; Female Christ references a 1969 performance piece by Danish artist Lene Adler Petersen, in which she walked naked through the Copenhagen Stock Exchange carrying a cross. For the Costner collection, they’ve developed a group of three scents, titled After Hours, Pacific Coast Highway, and Heritage, all based on the actor’s life.

After Hours, the signature scent of the collection, has notes of cypress, bourbon, and nutmeg; Bergelin describes as a version of 19-69’s Rainbow Bar fragrance “on steroids,” and says that for him, it evokes the image of someone in a glass house in the Hollywood Hills at sunset, surveying the city. It reminds Costner of shooting one of his first major magazine covers, the May 1989 issue of Vanity Fair, with the louche, legendary Helmut Newton. “So, I was in a tuxedo but my tie was off and my jacket was on the couch,” he recounts. “It was supposed to be a sexy shot with a glass of whiskey. And Helmut kept saying, ‘take your shirt off, just leave the bow tie on or something’... I said no. I’m not an exotic dancer!” He carefully notes that Newton was “so sweet” and did not make this suggestion in any sort of predatory fashion. He was merely opposed to looking like a Chippendale.

Costner and 19-69 co-founder Johan Bergelin.

Costner once wore After Hours to one of his son’s volleyball tournaments. “There were about 15 teams of teenage boys,” he says. “So the gym smelled really bad.” He says a pregnant woman kept staring at him, with her friend tugging her arm, urging her not to approach. “She walks all across the gym towards me,” he says. “And I wasn’t sure what this was going to be about. And she just turns to me and says, ‘Look, I’m married, I’m not [trying to bother you]. But what are you wearing?’” She did not ask for an autograph.

“The scent doesn’t have to be obvious,” he says. “But I love that it somehow becomes noticeable on its own merit, period.”

Pacific Coast Highway is lighter, with notes of clementine, juniper berry, and jasmine, Bergelin’s favorite. It smells like going to the beach in California (on a day without an algae bloom or whatever) and then taking a long hot shower after. It is clean without being boring, and I love it. Costner says the PCH scent reminds him of Tommy James and the Shandell’s 1968 hit “Crystal Blue Persuasion,” a lysergic earworm inspired by the Books of Isaiah, Ezekiel, and Revelations that most listeners and critics instead took to be a song about acid, amphetamines, or meth. “It’s easy,” he says. “A light sensory moment.”

Heritage is a rich, masculine scent that nods to life on Costner’s sprawling ranch in Aspen, Colorado, with notes of cherry, violet leaves, amber, and vanilla, scents that sound sweet and girly on paper but add up to something whiskey-ish (“It’s on the richer side,” says Bergelin. “It has volume.”) Costner says it reminds him of “a place that’s very masculine, carpets and wood and guns and there’s a deepness to it like the wood of a place. It has a forest-y feeling to me.”

Costner, an avid hunter, is eager to talk about guns as we sniff Heritage. He both believes in laws that limit them and sees them as heirlooms, like jewelry. “If you misuse a gun, you should never have one again,” he says. “But I have my father’s gun, and he got it when he was young. And so, when I put that gun to my cheek, I can feel him. When I was a boy, I would just ask him to let me hold it and it was much too heavy for me. And now it feels almost light as a feather. And my sons will one day put the gun to their cheek when I won’t be here, and they’ll remember those times in the field with that dog and everything else.”

19-69’s tagline is “bottling counterculture.” But Costner, born in 1955, had a different experience of the hippie years. He was raised in a conservative household without much money in Southern California; his late brother left to serve in the Marines in Vietnam while other kids his age protested the war. He rigorously studied classical piano with an elderly instructor who had been around long enough to teach FDR. “You can imagine where the politics were with my mother and father, who wanted to support their son in Vietnam,” he says. “At the dinner table, long hair was bad. The music was bad. They didn’t like anything about it. And I was sitting there eating at that same table thinking I liked a lot of it.”

He was too young to go full Woodstock. But he remembers what it was like to be around the culture. “A whole generation here went north to the Haight-Ashbury, to San Francisco, the Summer of Love,” he remembers. “I would watch the last exit out of Santa Barbara when there was no freeway. It was just a highway with lights, and the last one was like a last man out of Saigon moment. There would sometimes be as many as 60, 70 kids at that light with their thumbs out.”

Costner’s best-known roles are visceral and sexy, and in watching them, it’s easy to conjure up scents: saddle leather, the grass of the Great Plains and baseball parks, sweat, campfires, gunpowder. Millions of people have formed their own relationships with him by going to the movies, surrounded by the scents of melted butter and spilled soda. One 2016 study examined the olfactory experience of going to the cinema, in which chemists asserted that audiences emitted different compounds, like carbon dioxide and isoprene, depending on the genre of film they were watching, unwittingly creating new scents for emotional or romantic scenes. If that’s true, which seems awfully nice to believe, Costner has, in one small way, been in the fragrance business for a long time.

He says his favorite smells are lemon, cinnamon, and sage (“I knew [sage] as a hunter, not as an in-crowd thing”), and his house smells like cleanliness, or nothing. I asked if he likes the smell of movie theaters. “Not particularly,” he says. “I think what I miss in a theater is the curtain. But I know that smell of popcorn. It’s almost ceremonial, in a way, and it’s always lousy. It’s better when you cook it at home, it just is.”

The Bald Guy Glow-Up Only Happens When You Accept Your Hair Loss

Crying in the Club (and Everywhere Else) With Jordan Firstman

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

“This is what you’re keeping me from right now,” Jordan Firstman says, and pivots his laptop screen to face a high hotel window overlooking a long, tempting stretch of Brazilian beach.

Firstman is in Rio de Janeiro for work, presenting three film-festival screenings of Club Kid, his directorial debut, which he also wrote and stars in as Peter, a dissolute downtown New York club promoter whose own extended adolescence ends abruptly when he’s called upon to parent Arlo, the 10-year-old son he’s never met.

The Brazilian audiences “really got it,” Firstman reports. It seems like everybody who watches Club Kid really gets it. The film received a seven-minute ovation at the Cannes Film Festival back in May, and sold to A24 for a reported $17 million after a brief, buzz-stoking bidding war. Preview screenings in LA, where Firstman lives, have been packed to the rafters.

Shirt and pants by Brioni. Bow tie and cummerbund, stylist’s own. Vintage watch by Rolex from Foundwell. Vintage cufflinks by Cartier from Foundwell. Earring, his own.

But when it’s suggested that this all bodes well for Club Kid’s broader appeal when it finally hits theaters, Firstman says, “Only time will tell. I hope it translates to the gen-pop.”

What we do know: Club Kid is hands down the most heartwarming film ever made about a man raising a child conceived during a blotto bisexual threesome—a supremely confident comedy-drama, raw and honest about gay sex, party drugs, and parenting in a way that’s excitingly and delightfully at odds with the sweetness of its tone. It’s an extremely likeable film, and its director-star is extremely likeable in it. And, I’ll say—at the risk of being rude—this means it’s kind of a departure for Firstman, who up until now has cultivated an almost proudly polarizing comedic persona online and onscreen. “I think, when you’re annoying and gay, people can’t get past that, in our culture,” he says, “and it’s interesting to me.”

Firstman is 35, and has not been famous particularly long, but if his public life so far has taught him anything, it’s how quickly the wind can shift. He broke through doing silly front-facing video sketches during COVID—playing banana bread’s publicist, yass-ing up his new favorite client, that sort of thing—and before long found himself apologizing for unearthed edgelord tweets he’d written in his early 20s. (He said in a statement that he’d “grown a million lifetimes” since those posts.)

His run on Fox’s English Teacher ended with the show’s cancellation in 2025; around the same time, he popped off to Vulture about the sex scenes in Heated Rivalry, suggesting the hockey bromance didn’t have a clue about “how gay people fuck.” (He is perhaps the only person ever to have pissed off that show’s fan base and lived to tell the tale, which may be evidence that nothing can stop his rise.)

Now ensconced once again as a TV-series regular—on HBO’s I Love LA—and with a buzzy movie in the pipeline, he’s able to look at all this with sunrise/sunset serenity.

“There’s ebbs and flows to whether people fuck with you or not,” he says. “Everyone loved me during the pandemic, and then everyone hated me for a little bit, and now it feels like people like me again.”

Still, he remains, by his own admission, “too online,” unable to not read things written about him, and it’s hard not to internalize some of that stuff, a little bit. He was gay and annoying in 2023’s Rotting in the Sun, playing “Jordan Firstman,” a dick-swinging, clout-chasing imp who torments a depressive artist played by the film’s director, Sebastián Silva. No less an authority than Pedro Almodóvar saw the movie and told Firstman he was a “real actor,” but his was one of the kinder reactions.

“I kind of assumed audiences would understand it was a performance,” Firstman says, “and I think many didn’t.” He thinks the movie is brilliant and calls Silva a genius. But he’s also convinced that playing a maximally annoying caricature of himself “really did affect the way people saw me, and then it affected how I saw myself. The year after Rotting was actually very tough for me, because I felt very hated.”

The new film, he says, is partly a response to that experience, he says. “I think Club Kid came from a place of, ‘I know how I feel, and I know how I’m being perceived is so different than how I feel, and I want to show a more true side of myself through this work.’”

That wasn’t how it started, of course. It started with Firstman at a dinner party, doing bits with his friends’ kid. That was the spark—Firstman realizing that putting a Jordan Firstman–type character, talking the way he does, in situations with an impressionable minor who maybe shouldn’t hear the kinds of things Firstman tends to say out loud, could be a great comedic setup.

Club Kid is in conversation with a long line of films about characters thrust into a parental role they’re ill-equipped for; Firstman can rattle them off, from Chaplin’s The Kid to Auntie Mame to Kramer vs. Kramer to Big Daddy. But as he wrote, bigger ideas emerged—about the “arrested development” of a certain cohort of childless gay men, about what it actually means to be an adult. And in working out how Peter would show up to his newfound relationship with Arlo, he thought about his own parents—two Newsday reporters who raised him in the New York suburbs—and what he got from them, as well as what he wished he’d gotten.

“I feel like no one taught me what life was going to be like. We just got by in a pleasant-ish way. But I don’t think I got major insights,” he says. A beat. “It is what it is. I am grateful that I went looking for those insights in my early 20s, through drug trips.”

This is what it’s like to talk to Firstman these days; he’s reflective, but he’ll still spring a provocative overshare on you like a trap door. (Boing! Drug trips!) The first time we met, at a photo shoot a few weeks before the call from Brazil, he told me that although the standing ovation thing at Cannes is overblown (“It felt great, but it’s such a ruse. It’s like, you can keep it going if you want. You just don’t let it stop. [From the stage] You keep pointing at different people in the movie”), the experience of showing the movie to an appreciative audience at the most prestigious film festival in the world had been transformative, that it felt like “20 years of validation that I didn’t get,” and that he no longer feels the same need to “say the edgy thing” all the time.

But at the very beginning of that conversation—as I remember it, we were still in the process of shaking hands—he started telling me an off-the-record story involving, let’s say, an adult situation. Today, over Zoom, I ask if that anecdote (explicit, slightly alarming) was the old Jordan briefly reasserting himself by saying the edgy thing.

“It probably came from a place of, maybe, nervousness, if I’m being honest,” he says. “But I think, probably, there was some sort of provocation that was a bit of a shield in that story.”

And it’s more complicated than that, he notes—because the guy who says the edgy thing is a part of him, too, and who’s to say it’s not his best self? He really does feel, after Cannes, like he doesn’t have to prove himself in quite the same way, he says.

“But then I’m like, ‘Oh, am I changing into something that’s less honest, or more honest? Are the gritty parts of me honest, and I’m letting go of them to be more palatable?’” Or is it a defense mechanism, “and the real me is something softer and sweeter and more put-together?”

The goal, of course, is to amalgamate all our disparate selves. Wouldn’t that be great? Firstman has tried his best.

“I’ve done a lot,” he says. “Everything mystical, I’ve tried.”

He’s tried past-life regression, and he’s tried ayahuasca, and he’s the second person I’ve interviewed—the first being Mike Tyson—who has tried bufotenin, a hallucinogen found in the venom of the Sonoran desert toad. (He doesn’t recall much about the first time he tried it, except a feeling “like something really bad happened”; the second time was “a way more positive, beautiful experience.”)

“I think I’ve always been a very curious person,” he says. “They say with ayahuasca, it finds you when you’re supposed to do it.”

When he heard about ayahuasca—a traditional psychoactive tea brewed from two plants native to the Amazon—on comedian Duncan Trussell’s podcast, he says, “It felt like I got shot in the stomach. I was like, ‘I have to do this.’” The next day he was at a party and somebody told him they’d just done it, under the supervision of a shaman in Topanga. Firstman went on to do it nine times over the course of his 20s.

“By the end, it kicked me out,” he says. “God, literally, was like, ‘You can’t come back here. This realm is not for you, ever again.’ I still did it one more time after that, and it was like, Bitch, are you serious? It almost really fucked me up.”

So he was pushing it, he reasons, with the toad venom. “I maybe shouldn’t have even done that, but I think it’s a different thing.”

He has this secret spiritual tool kit now, of stuff he’s learned from these experiences, about his own perceptions, and he thinks it’s protected him. He wasn’t looking for a way out of anything or relief from any particular suffering or struggle. He wanted to learn about life and what there was to experience—which, as it happens, is also how he’s approached his working life.

“I do it through film, I do it through music. I do it through social interactions. I think life is about learning in any way you can and experiencing everything you can.”

Instagram videos. Sitcoms. Directing a movie. Each another trip: What happens when I do this? He figures he could probably have built a more stable career, “if I had a more diligent respect for one medium, maybe.” But he can’t help where his creative impulses take him. Was anybody clamoring for Firstman to make 2025’s Secrets, a full-length comedy album of oddly well-executed genre-parody songs with titles like “I’m Hoarding Cum” and “Pennies in My Pussy”?

The answer is no. (The actual answer is probably “not at all.”) But once he started making the record, Firstman says, “there was no stopping me. I was in the studio, like, ‘What am I doing in a music studio?’ But I couldn’t not. I was there every day. I was begging any producer I knew to help me, because I just needed to.”

Chances are you could not make a movie like Club Kid, whose realness stems in part from a clearly lived-in understanding of partying, without having done some wild shit. But it would be difficult to make this movie if you were still living that way. Peter has Arlo to drop-kick him out of suspended adolescence and into adulthood; I ask Firstman what the catalyst was for his own growth.

“I think it was a slow process,” he says, “that’s still happening, to some degree.”

Some of what happened was professional: In 2025, he put out the album and shot the second season of English Teacher, the first season of I Love LA, and Club Kid. The practical demands of that schedule forced him to lock in: “I didn’t have Saturdays off to party, and I couldn’t afford, on a Monday, to not feel like my best.”

He also went through a breakup “that was really, really, really sad and just really unexpected, and someone I never thought I would break up with.”

He doesn’t want to be too specific about, y’know, the specifics. But it was a split he initiated.

“It came to a point where I was like, ‘I know the life I want to live and I don’t think this love is aligning with that life.’ I had to choose my life over love—which is such a mind-fuck, because you’re like, ‘Love is everything. There’s nothing more important than love.’ But then, your life is also—on a day-to-day level, it’s pretty important. And so yeah, I think it was a really tough, bizarre out-of-body experience almost, because it was a love I had never known the likes of before, and then it was over.”

By the time they broke up they had been together for four years. When it started, Firstman says, he couldn’t believe they’d found each other and the depth of what he was feeling for this person. “And I felt the exact same way with the breakup,” he says. “I was like, ‘I cannot believe this is happening.’ It feels so unreal. It just doesn’t make any sense. As much as the love didn’t make sense in the beginning, how powerful it felt, the breakup didn’t. It was the exact same feeling.

After their last conversation they agreed to stay friends, and then they didn’t talk for a year. “I think he was protecting himself and maybe protecting me too,” Firstman says. When his ex finally responded to a message, Firstman was on set, about to shoot a climactic scene in Club Kid—he’d scripted, as it happens, a big emotional moment for Peter, a moment where two characters connect on the phone after a long time apart.

“The first time, in a year, he had responded, was the moment before they called ‘Action,’” Firstman says. “That’s why I cried so hard in that scene, just like a huge release.”

His ex hasn’t seen the movie yet, Firstman says, and maybe he never will. It’s dedicated to him, though—his name is in the end credits. “He doesn’t know that either,” he says. “I’m a little scared. I’m a little scared, because to me, the movie is a love letter to him.”

He starts crying here, a little bit, apologizing even as it’s happening.

“All of that extra love that I still have for him, I put it directly into the movie. If he does see it, which I don’t expect him to or need him to, I hope he sees that’s where all my love went, and that I still have it for him and I always will.”

He has not started writing his next movie yet, but he’s started thinking about it. He knows that like this one, it’ll be a love story—and that after writing, in Club Kid, about familial love, community love, and loving yourself, he wants to write about friendship next.

In the past year, he’s been blown away by two movies of vast scope, Paul Thomas Anderson’s One Battle After Another (“I was making my movie when that came out, and I think that is a movie, for him, about growing up, and not feeling like a grown-up as an adult”) and the equally sprawling queer epic La Bola Negra. I ask him if he’s looking to go similarly big with his next project. Maybe, he says.

“But I think I’d like to take incremental steps and learn more as a filmmaker before.…”

“Yeah,” he says, laughing. “My war movie. The Firstman war movie. I have zero context for war, so I would be completely making everything up.” ✦

I Tried the NordicTrack Ultra 1 Reformer—Here’s My Honest Thoughts

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Everyone remembers their first time on a reformer, or at least I do. After months of mat Pilates—with a strengthened core and improved flexibility—I thought it was finally time to take things up a notch. Mat sessions, in my naive opinion, were the safer option. Though now I’d argue the right mat class can be significantly tougher, at the time, taking my talents to an actual reformer felt like an uphill athletic battle. Was I ready to handle the challenge of a weighted workout atop a strange machine that could, theoretically, twist me into a pretzel or fling me across the room? With some trepidation, I eventually booked my first reformer class; on my birthday, no less. What better time to try something new?

Fortunately, I quickly learned that reformers are not as intimidating as they seem. Through the instructor’s patient cueing and hands-on adjustments, I quickly got the hang of it. I left the class with an energizing sense of accomplishment, and a Pilates bug that wouldn’t soon die down. Over the next few years, I’ve gone on to take too many classes to count. With my bank account depleted (let’s be real, keeping up with consistent classes is expensive), I wondered if there was a suitable at-home solution. That’s precisely when the NordicTrack Ultra 1 Reformer came across my desk; or rather, was delivered to the World Trade Center’s studio for me to give it a proper test. Read on below to see how the machine works, and if it lives up to a Pilates princess’s expectations.

Launched in May 2026, the Ultra 1 is NordicTrack’s second reformer (the Ultra 1 RX-S launched in 2025); an obvious next chapter for the exercise equipment brand, as Pilates interest continues to grow globally. “We saw a need to create a connected Pilates studio experience that anyone could enjoy at home with a reformer that felt more like high-end furniture than fitness equipment,” Mandy Crane, NordicTrack’s VP, head of brand and product marketing, tells Vogue. “The Ultra 1 Reformer sits at the intersection of excellence in performance and design.”

What you’ll first notice about the Ultra 1 is that it’s a treat to the eyes. Easily one of the most aesthetically pleasing reformers I’ve ever seen (versus reformers I've seen in studio and at home), this is crafted with sculpted form, soft edges, and under-side ambient lighting that makes it a statement piece in any room you place it. This, the brand tells me, is precisely what they were going for. “It's a piece of equipment you don't feel the need to hide away; you want it to be part of your home,” Fit Pilates product marketing director Yvette McGaffin says. “For me, that's an important part of wellness through design: creating something that is as considered aesthetically as it is in the way it supports how you move and feel.”

McGaffin, a Pilates instructor and studio owner herself, continues that every design decision serves a purpose, as do the details of the experience itself. “Traditional reformers can involve a learning curve around spring combinations, manual adjustments and the mechanics of the machine. The Ultra 1 brings together studio-grade equipment, push-button resistance, and iFIT-guided instruction to make that experience more intuitive and connected.”

Most importantly, NordicTrack wanted the Ultra 1 to suit different body types and experience levels. Though I am not a beginner, I was admittedly a bit hesitant using the machine without a certified instructor present. However, there are a few things in place to eliminate much of the guesswork. Of course, there’s the handy touchscreen that not only offers 75+ streamable reformer Pilates sessions, it also confirms the spring you’re on, flags when it doesn’t match the instructor’s cues, and reinforces the correct footbar position. That way, you can be more certain you’re on the right track. For more experienced users, McGaffin says it’s about expanding what’s possible: “The rear toggles support upper-body work against greater spring resistance, while the Smart Spine can be used as a stable platform for more advanced core, lunge and dynamic, athletic-style exercises,” she says. “

Once the device is in place, getting started is really as simple as plugging it in and toggling the on switch. From there, the lights will illuminate, and you can log in to your iFit account (or make one, obviously) to stream your workout.

The trickiest part of my entire experience, ironically, was situating the springs and footbars. To change the springs, your carriage must be firmly in the home position. If you’re having trouble, learn from me, and ensure the long strap is on the shoulder rest; mine was on the short strap, which pulled the carriage towards the other end of the frame. Once home, you can press the button located near the footbar to activate your desired resistance level. From left to right, you have Blue (33 lbs), Red (44 lbs), and Green (22 lbs); pick one or any combination of the three to your liking or as cued by your workout.

What’s nice, though, is that the spring system is enclosed, meaning you’ll never have to worry about hitting or falling on them throughout your workout. According to McGaffin, this is its Smart Spine framing. “The push-button controls also remove the need to manually change springs, helping simplify setup for people who are new to reformer Pilates and allowing them to focus more on learning the movement.”

The footbars, on the other hand, have four positions available. To change position, gently lift and push or pull to the desired angle. Once locked in place, the footbar position and resistance level will appear in the top right of your screen.

Choosing your workout is genuinely as simple as scrolling through the options, and choosing the right one for you. Certain options, marked by experience level from beginner to advanced, are available as a series, meaning you can do one workout at a time (ideally, per day) for the full effect. Some standouts include Sweat & Sculpt, Powerhouse Performance, and the Aligned Athlete series. Generally, each session lasts about 45 to 50 minutes.

To get warmed up, I started with iFit’s Reformer Essentials program, which further walked me through how to get the most out of the Ultra 1. After that, I took my time looking through the vast library of offerings. Throughout my test, I most enjoyed the Sculpt & Fly series, instructed by McGaffin, to build strength and stamina. While some classes can focus on subtle movements and intensive stretching, this one was much more powerful. It felt like a combination of cardio-meets-Pilates that helped tone, but also break a sweat.

As this class utilizes the jumpboard and Pilates box, with specific exercises catered to the full body, you do move around a lot throughout the duration of a session. That said, because no one was cueing me to the next move or improving my form, I found that I tried to keep my eyes glued to the screen. Fortunately, the screen has a good range of movement; save for when I faced the back of the reformer, I had no issues keeping up.

Overall, I found the Ultra 1 seamless to use. The glide on this thing is incredible—the carriage moves smoothly, and is quite comfortable to exercise on. For reference, I’m 5’2 and did not have any issues whether I was lying down, using the straps, or pushing off the footbar. Sometimes I had trouble locating the straps during pose transitions, but that’s normal for any class. The entire machine weighs about 300 lbs, which is on the heavier side, and it features wood accents and a very stable steel-frame structure. Where weight can be of concern is if you’re moving it around: it’s for sure a two-person job, even to rotate it from one side of the room to another.

The more workouts I completed, the more comfortable I felt with the machine. My biggest piece of advice would be, don’t force yourself to move too quickly—whether that’s pushing yourself to do the most challenging movements or complete the most advanced series. McGaffin offers similar guidance: “Reformer Pilates offers something quite special because it can be both a physical challenge and a chance to switch off from everything else,” she says. “My advice is not to get caught up in how an exercise looks or how much resistance you're using. Stay connected to your breath, move with intention and pay attention to how your body feels. That's where the real mind-body connection develops.”
