20 Steals From Backcountry’s Early Black Friday Sale

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

I love Black Friday. I also hate Black Friday. Sure, you can snag unbelievable bargains on outdoor gear on what’s become the national holiday of capitalism, but it’s also easy to get lost in the digital vortex. There are just so many sales to choose from, and often those sales are limited to a brief window. Decision paralysis and exhaustion ensue—even if you haven’t loaded up on tryptophan-laced turkey.

That said, I’m happy to report that Backcountry’s Early Black Friday sale starts today, November 6th. While the online outfitter will add heaps of gear to the sale as Black Friday approaches, the early sale gives bargain hunters weeks to shop.

Even better? Right now, Backcountry is offering up to 20% off full-price items and an extra 10 to 20% off clearance, from high-end brands like The North Face, Blundstone, Dakine, Black Diamond, and more. No code necessary, just add your wish list gear and the discount shows up in your cart when you’re signed in to your (free) Summit Club Membership. And if you’re a Summit+ Member, you get even more savings, like an extra $50 cash back on certain deals.

This is pretty cool. I write about the best gear sales every month for Outside, and this discount code gives shoppers a rare opportunity to scoop up current gear (not last season’s inventory) at a double-digit discount. To celebrate, I rounded up 20 picks from Backcountry’s Early Black Friday Sale, all of which I’ve personally tested or am currently putting on the chopping block here in Tahoe.

Long story short: keep reading for my top picks or check out the sale in its entirety here.

Three decades after its debut, The North Face dug into the archives and dropped an alpine-ready version of the iconic Nuptse Jacket (available in both men’s and women’s styles). Generously stuffed with 800-fill goose down (Backcountry’s current product description says 700-fill, but The North Face claims higher-grade 800-fill down here), the Summit Nuptse puffy is as warm as they come. I love the boxy cut and classic color-blocked colorways—style elements that are at home in mountain towns and metros alike. In my opinion, this puffy is overkill on the slopes, but I wouldn’t hesitate to recommend it as a winter camping basecamp parka or a belay puffy. That said, you can also deploy it as a daily winter essential, whether you’re partaking in parking lot après at your local ski resort or on a frigid dog walk.

This pairing of the Smith Method Pro MIPS helmet and the Squad MAG goggle has everything I’m looking for in a helmet-and-goggle combo. The helmet offers solid protection thanks to a MIPS liner and in-mold construction, while the grid-style ear pads are comfortable and removable to boot. I love the low-profile silhouette of the helmet, the built-in ventilation, and the quick-and-easy magnetic buckle. It’s also light enough for me to bring on backcountry tours. The Method Pro’s flat brim fits seamlessly with the Squad MAG, a stylish cylindrical goggle that features Smith’s streamlined lens interchangeability system and top-of-the-line ChromaPop contrast-enhancing lens tech. Separately, I’m a big fan of these Smith options, but together, they’re a dynamic duo that can seriously amp up your on-hill enjoyment.

I’ve never had a pair of Smartwool base layers that I haven’t liked—and that’s saying something, as I’ve tested enough of the brand’s thermal merino offerings over the last 15 years to have a fresh set of long johns for every day of the week. One of the alluring aspects of merino wool, however, is the natural fiber’s antimicrobial and anti-odor properties, so if you’re traveling, camping, or only have one set, you can wear these Smartwool pieces multiple days in a row without inciting the ire of your ski buddies. These base layers are crafted from 100% merino wool, and the mid-heavyweight fabric is toasty and ready for sub-zero days on the slopes.

I just got my hands on this Norrona Lofoten Gore-Tex Pro kit last week, and I took it out during the first snowfall of the season here in Tahoe. It withstood fat, sopping flakes with no problem—unsurprising considering that it’s crafted with Gore-Tex Pro, what many (myself included) consider to be the best-in-class membrane for skiing and snowboarding. I was surprised, however, by how much I dug the style (the two-tone red is unabashedly fly), and I love how the pant and jacket zip together for extra defense on the deepest of days. I can’t offer anything beyond these first impressions right now, but if you’re in the market for premium outerwear—and want to save some money while you’re at it—this Scandinavian alpine armor merits a closer look.

While I appreciate the breathability of a softshell kit for touring, I love to chase powder and shred through storm cycles. I also call the moisture-prone Sierra home. As such, I often gravitate toward a less breathable but more waterproof hardshell. Enter Norrona’s More outerwear—a featherweight option that skintrack regulars will appreciate.

The Gore-Tex weatherproofing is on point when winter storm warnings roll in, while the 40D face fabric and a no-frills feature set keep weight low. It’s not the most durable jacket in my closet, but it’s one of the most capable in its weight class and one I reach for on backyard adventures and international trips alike. The low-slung bib is a highlight of the kit, too: the cut is high enough to keep powder from soaking your base layers, but low enough that you won’t overheat as much while touring.

Gram-counting backpackers won’t even consider this Therm-a-Rest NeoLoft Sleeping Pad. But if you prioritize comfort over pack weight, the 4.6-inch-thick NeoLoft is worth considering, especially if you split time between moderate backpacking missions and car camping adventures. My favorite feature is the curvature of the side rails, which keep you from falling off the comfy, supportive, well-insulated sleeping pad onto cold, hard ground.

The tried-and-true Wawona tent series has long been a favorite of Outside staff, but the new Universal 3-person version is an entirely fresh addition to the lineup. Essentially, it’s designed using “universal design” principles, which seek to improve outdoor accessibility and inclusivity. Noteworthy additions on that front include a massive, floor-to-ceiling vestibule that can fit two wheelchairs and a leash attachment for service dogs. This tent is truly universal, though, as any campers will appreciate the vertical space, durable materials, and painless setup.

Equal parts fire pit, grill, and campground centerpiece, Snow Peak’s Takibi will change the way you camp more than any other piece of gear in this guide. Forged from stainless steel, the Japanese-made fire pit is built to last. While the heirloom-quality build isn’t lightweight, intentional design makes folding, transporting, and storing it relatively chill. The raised, removable grill is a welcome tool if you’re looking to get into open-fire cooking—one best paired with wagyu steaks, fresh-caught trout, sizzling prawns, and other such delicacies. And Snow Peak makes veritable mountains of accessories, so you can customize your Takibi setup to your camp cooking needs.

I love DB’s Snow Roller Pro for a few reasons. First off, an internal “ribcage” protects contents in transit. The versatile roller handles both skis and boards thanks to an adjustable length that fits 203-centimeter skis and chubby, volume-shifted snowboards. The wheels are beefy, and the handle connects to the DB 90L Hugger Roller Bag for near-effortless one-handed steering. But as someone with limited gear storage at home, my favorite feature is that the Snow Roller Pro rolls up into a compact, easy-to-stash bundle when you’re not using it. All told, it’s a welcome addition to any powder-chaser’s arsenal, and a great gift for anyone you tolerate enough to invite on a ski trip.

This rolling Black Hole Wheeled Duffel features the same tester-beloved armored fabric, pocket configuration, and heavy-duty lash points. However, it’s much more expensive, but you get a ton of utility for the extra outlay, with oversized wheels and a telescoping handle for easy maneuvering in international airports and crowded subway stations. This is my go-to for powder pilgrimages to Japan, British Columbia, you name it.

The Numbers Game: Man Utd look to build on Champions League against in-form Villa

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

All eyes are once again on Manchester United as they look to turn around their Premier League form against Aston Villa before the international break.

United were winless in nine games ahead of Tuesday's meeting with Roma in the Champions League, and things looked to be getting worse when they found themselves 2-0 down after 17 minutes.

However, they orchestrated a superb second-half comeback to win 3-2 and earn their first victory since September 10, thanks to Matheus Cunha's late brace, and ease some of the pressure on Michael Carrick's shoulders.

But with the threat of dropping into the relegation zone now a very real worry, the test is to translate that form into the top flight against a Villa side on the way up the rankings.

After failing to win any of their first four league games, they are now unbeaten in five (W3 D2), though they come into this match on the back of a 3-0 thrashing at the hands of Barcelona in the Champions League.

This will be Unai Emery's 200th Premier League game as a manager, though, and Villa will want to make sure he celebrates the landmark on a high.

So, who will come out on top at Old Trafford on Sunday? Here, using Opta data, we look at the key storylines.

— Manchester United (@ManUtd) November 5, 2026

There is not much between these teams according to the Opta supercomputer, though United are narrow favourites to clinch all three points, winning 39% of the 10,000 pre-match simulations.

That is helped by the fact that United have only lost two of their 31 Premier League home games against Villa (W24 D5), 1-0 defeats in both 2009-10 and 2021-22.

Villa won their first Premier League match against the Red Devils under Emery in November 2022, but they have won just one of their seven since then (D1 L5).

That victory came in December last year at Villa Park, thanks to Morgan Rogers' brace, but they then lost the reverse fixture 3-1 in March.

Emery's side are still given a 34.5% chance of triumphing this weekend, as they look for a third straight win on the road in the top flight.

There is still a 26.5% chance of a draw, which means a stalemate is fairly likely, but it does not feel like a point will be enough for United this weekend.

The first 17 minutes against Roma seemed to sum up all of United's recent struggles, and they were lucky not to be further behind when the half-time whistle blew.

But they were a vastly different team after the break, helped by Roma's complacency, as Cunha led the charge, winning the penalty from which Bruno Fernandes scored at the second attempt, before bagging himself a brace.

He then got himself sent off with two quickfire bookings – a needless late tackle after excessive celebrations – but his hard work had already got the job done.

4 – No team have won more UEFA Champions League games from two or more goals behind than Manchester United (also Real Madrid). Salvation. https://t.co/sclavosZVa

United's confidence will be buoyed by the dramatics of Tuesday's victory, and Carrick will be looking to replicate the second-half dominance. 10 of their 15 shots against Roma came after the break, with 2.5 of their 2.7 total expected goals (xG) generated coming after the interval.

However, their league form is a very real worry as United are winless in their last seven Premier League games (D4 L3).

Only between April and May 2025 have they had a longer run without winning in the competition (eight games), which was ended by a 2-0 home win over Villa.

Indeed, the Red Devils have just one win in nine Premier League games this season (D4 L4). They have not started a league season with one win in 10 games since 1972-73 (W1 D4 L5).

Only three teams in top-flight history have followed a season where they finished in the top three with as few as one win in their first 10 games – Derby County in 1894-95, Chelsea in 1920-21, and Arsenal in 1953-54.

There are issues at both ends of the pitch for Carrick to address, but their defensive frailty has proven to be their biggest weakness.

United are one of only two sides without a Premier League clean sheet this season, along with Ipswich Town. It is the longest into a league season they have gone without a clean sheet since 1986-87 (10 games).

The last side to finish in the top three of the top flight, then fail to keep a clean sheet in their first 10 games of the next season, were Liverpool in 1966-67.

United have conceded 15 goals in total in the league, with five of those coming across their last two losses against Bournemouth (3-0) and Chelsea (2-0), respectively.

There were signs of positivity in the first half against Chelsea as well, before a second-half capitulation, and consistency across 90 minutes is another concern that needs to be fixed if United want their first league win since MD2 of the competition.

While there were jubilant celebrations at Old Trafford on Tuesday, Villa endured a much different ending against LaLiga champions Barcelona.

It was their first defeat in Europe this season and saw them suffer their joint-heaviest defeat in major European competition, having also lost by three goals to Royal Antwerp in September 1975 (1-4) and Inter in November 1990 (0-3), both in the UEFA Cup.

Moreover, no Premier League side has committed more errors leading to goals in all competitions this season than Villa (seven, level with Sunderland).

But while Emery was frustrated that his side were not more clinical against Barcelona, he will not be dwelling on the result for too long as they aim to continue their rise up the Premier League.

Their five-game unbeaten streak has lifted them up to 10th in the table, and Emery, who is reportedly in advanced talks over a new Villa deal, will be keen to continue their upward trajectory in his landmark match.

He is the fifth Spaniard to reach 200 matches in the Premier League after Rafael Benitez, Roberto Martinez, Pep Guardiola, and Mikel Arteta, with Benitez's (2-0 win) and Arteta's (1-1 draw) 200th games also coming against United.

Unai Emery's first game in charge of the Villa, #OnThisDay four years ago  https://t.co/hpiYbgtARQ

— Aston Villa (@AstonVilla) November 6, 2026

There are good signs that they can come away with all three points, as well, given Villa have won their last two away league matches, as many as in their previous 12 combined (D5 L5). They last had a longer run between November and December 2025 (four).

And a fast start could be the key to letting the nerves creep back in at Old Trafford, given United's recent record.

Villa have scored in the first half of each of their last five Premier League games, having gone five games in a row beforehand without a goal in the first 45 minutes.

The Villans last scored in the first half of six league games in a row between May and August 2023.

In 2026, Fernandes ranks first for each of chances created (104), open-play chances created (80), big chances created (27), successful final-third passes (531), passes/crosses played into the opposition box (289), and throughballs (29).

He is averaging 3.8 chances created per 90 minutes under Carrick, the best by any United player under a single manager in the league since 2003-04 (min. 1,000 mins played).

Since his United debut in February 2020, Fernandes has both taken (50) and scored (44) the most penalties of any Premier League player across all competitions (excluding shoot-outs), after his spot-kick against Roma.

Nicolas Jackson has scored five goals in eight Premier League appearances for Villa.

He is only the fourth Villa player to reach five goals in that few appearances, along with Dean Saunders in 1992 (four), Dion Dublin in 1998 (two) and Ollie Watkins in 2020 (seven).

Across all competitions, Jackson has netted eight goals from 35 shots and is averaging a goal every 121 minutes. His 22.9% shot conversion rate is also the third-highest in the squad.

Crypto ETFs see over half a billion dollars in outflows amid post-midterm market slide

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

U.S. spot crypto ETFs saw more than $500 million in combined net outflows on Thursday as crypto and tech markets fell across the board.

U.S. spot crypto exchange-traded funds witnessed more than half a billion dollars in net outflows on Thursday as markets reversed initial gains following the U.S. midterms election results.

Among bitcoin ETFs, Fidelity's FBTC led Thursday's net outflows with $199.9 million, followed by BlackRock's IBIT with $105 million and Grayscale's GBTC with $49.9 million, according to data compiled by The Block. Franklin Templeton's EZBC, VanEck's HODL, and Ark Invest's ARKB also saw net outflows, while the remaining ETFs posted zero flows for the day.

Ethereum funds also witnessed heavy net outflows, led by BlackRock's ETHA and ETHB, with $75.2 billion and $22 billion in redemptions, respectively. Fidelity's FETH and Grayscale's ETHE saw outflows too.

Other major crypto ETFs didn't fare much better, accounting for size. U.S. Solana ETF saw a combined $1.2 million in net outflows for the day, Zcash ETFs $11 million, and Hyperliquid ETFs $3 million, according to SoSoValue data. XRP ETFs were the only outlier, with zero flows for the day.

Major U.S. crypto ETFs have now seen a combined net outflow of more than $1.1 billion so far this week.

Bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) fell 5.6% on Thursday, more than erasing an initial 4% gain on Wednesday following Tuesday's U.S. midterm elections. Ether Ethereum (ETH) was hit harder, falling 7.8% for the day, with the GMCI 30 index, representing a selection of the top 30 cryptocurrencies, dropping 6.4%.

Tech stocks also suffered, with the Nasdaq Composite falling 5.5%, and the S&P 500 dropping 4.4% as investors continue to weigh the impact of potential restrictions on the AI infrastructure buildout following the Democrats' sweep of both the House and Senate.

Kyle Rodda, senior financial market analyst at Capital.com, said the Democratic sweep added to concerns over the AI investment boom, with investors already questioning technology valuations and the pace of infrastructure spending.

"The regulatory and political risks arise at a time when the proverbial AI trade was already on shakier foundations," Rodda said, pointing to disappointing earnings and signs of slowing AI spending.

Axel Rudolph, chief technical analyst at IG, told The Block that bitcoin has been "dragged into the AI rout as investors rapidly unwind risk across technology and crypto markets."

Rudolph said bitcoin's correlation with technology stocks had left it exposed to the selloff, with more than $1 billion in leveraged crypto positions liquidated in 24 hours as the market continued to decline.

Friday's U.S. jobs report could complicate matters further for markets, particularly if the figures give the Federal Reserve more reason to raise rates.

Economists expect around 60,000 to 65,000 new jobs, according to Daniela Hathorn, senior market analyst at Capital.com. She warned that a stronger jobs report, particularly one showing higher wage growth, could send both Treasury yields and the dollar higher.

Despite the broader market weakness, André Dragosch, head of research for Europe at Bitwise, expects bitcoin to outperform U.S. equities as the AI trade unwinds.

Dragosch said bitcoin is now more sensitive to the dollar and expectations for monetary policy than to economic growth, and could decouple from stocks if financial conditions improve.

In Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert doubles down on its maximalism in audacious, faintly sinister style

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

And so we are back in Pywel: the super-sized and often super-pretty realm where the deserts are blood-red, the grass is a lurid shade of green, and Kliff and his merry gang of Greymanes seem to be pissed all the time. Yet what's this? Peace seems to have broken out across the region, which means our grizzled action hero is in a reflective mood.

At the outset of Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert's recently released DLC, I'm tasked with taking Kliff out for a quiet spot of moonlit fishing. The problem is, I seem to have forgotten how to actually fish - and if memory serves, perhaps I never learned because the system was so poorly explained in the base game. So I flail around for a full 15 minutes before eventually relenting and consulting Reddit on how to snag a bite.

You might reasonably assume that these types of basic explanatory wrinkles had been ironed out in the countless updates since Crimson Desert's release in March. Certainly, developer Pearl Abyss has gone to great effort elsewhere to streamline what was, at times, a frustratingly clunky experience (those menus at launch, oh god) while adding even more stuff, such as all-new cutscenes (to improve the story's legibility) and the ability to ride wolves and tigers (because why the hell not). But for all the tinkering and wholesale additions, Crimson Desert has retained its essential character: the menus remain a total faff and the narrative, which I started afresh some weeks ago, is still bafflingly incoherent (maybe even more than I initially appreciated).

It's into this churn of ongoing development that Pearl Abyss has produced Charting the Unknown, a DLC which adds even more elements to a game already buckling under the weight of mechanical overload. Now you can build ships and sail them around Pywel's azure-blue waters. You can dive below the waves and swim with the fishes. You can also fall in love, get married, and - should things not pan out - get divorced. The everything game has become, well, even more everything in quintessentially audacious style. On that note, just wait until you see some of its supermassive Evangelion-esque maritime bosses.

Though don't get too excited. Like the base game, the DLC is a mixed bag. First, shipfaring lacks any kind of drama. Islands are so spread out as to make the actual journey painfully boring: impressionistic oceanic voyaging à la Wind Waker this is not. The diving, though, is beautifully executed. Underwater locales are notably serene, free from many of the irritating distractions that plague ground traversal.

As for the new narrative threads involving all three playable characters - grizzled Kliff, Bayonetta-alike Damiane, and gentle giant orc Oongka - they're equally uneven. Kliff's is the most interesting, transplanting the player to a desert cloaked in a thick orange sandstorm that, without the aid of a working compass, must be mapped out using beacons resembling luminescent dongs. It's pleasingly disorientating, a rare example of Pearl Abyss pushing a kind of intentional design friction, rather than accidentally creating it by inadequately explaining how to interact with the game.

Alas, there are also bugs, one of which was game-halting: Damiane's steed wouldn't spawn when I needed to follow Kliff on horseback. It was a reminder that for all the visual splendour, Crimson Desert can often feel like it's being held together by the code equivalent of gaffer tape.

Everything controls about as well as you'd expect if you've played the base game, i.e. not very well at all. Kliff may be able to run on water and soar through the sky like a cross between Matrix star Neo and Jesus, but he remains a bastard to control for even the most basic tasks. Why can't I lock onto the object a foot away? Why is he doing a three-hit combo somersault through the air when all I tried to do is pull out his lantern? These issues are compounded by the new romance and socialising options that appear every time you target an NPC: Crimson Desert becomes even fiddlier as it introduces these additional layers.

As the hours pass by - as I run, gallop, and bound through the arresting landscape in a manner that is comparatively frictionless compared to the rest of the game - my mind begins to numb. For all the extraneous mechanical gubbins, missions remain brainlessly simple: cavort from one waypoint to another and do a few simple button presses, or crack a few skulls to chug the narrative along. But then perhaps a certain numbing is the point of Crimson Desert? At a time when the real world is markedly inhospitable (diminishing job prospects, the ongoing cost-of-living crisis, the looming threat of geopolitical meltdown), Pearl Abyss' epic swaddles the player like a swords-and-sorcery comfort blanket. It is a safe space to disappear into for hours. Leave your worldly woes at the start screen.

The impression this mash-up of a game appears to want to impart is generosity: here, Pearl Abyss seems to say, is a product capable of accommodating your every wish, will, and whim. It appears to have left no gameplay stone unturned in its ultimate quest to provide the player with total satisfaction - a kind of blissed-out contentment at the thought of never having to leave this almost divine realm.

But rather than generous, perhaps there is something nefarious about Crimson Desert's maximalist design philosophy. It strikes me again that this is the most uniquely modern title I've ever played, as if the Grand Theft Auto and Assassin's Creed franchises, countless MMORPGs (the genre in which Pearl Abyss cut its teeth), and many hyper-compulsive mobile titles were squished together in an attention-devouring final form. One so mightily captivating, so singularly consuming, that the player keeps playing until their lifeforce is depleted and they can play no more.

That seems to be the way these "forever" games curve: toward the player's surrender - of mind, body, and time. And at least until Grand Theft Auto 6 is released in a few weeks, Crimson Desert and its new DLC seem to be the purest expression yet of this type of thing. Wading through its sheer mass of hit-or-miss distractions (which could expand even more), the design ethos begins to grate. As if I'm stuck in an elaborate, endlessly bifurcating maze of hamster wheels, always busy, though never enjoying any discernible pay-off. And so, as its presentational lustre wears off further, and its eye-popping massiveness becomes less of a novelty, Crimson Desert begins to hit differently: much less high-fantasy heaven and more like hell.

Thomas Tuchel has explained why Cole Palmer was left out of the latest England squad, repeating his belief that the Chelsea star “misses too many opportunities to impress” for his country.

Palmer was on Friday the headline omission from Tuchel’s 25-man squad to contest this month’s Nations League fixtures against Croatia and Spain.

NYSE-backed crypto venture faces 90% stock rejection as $92 liquidity problem threatens tokenization dream

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Apple, Nvidia and 65 other companies have objected to OKXICE's plans, while thin liquidity across existing tokenized stock markets raises a second major obstacle to its launch.

More than 90% of the stocks targeted by OKX and NYSE-backed tokenization venture now face objections from their issuers.

A comparison of OKXICE's latest regulatory notice with its original October proposal shows that companies have objected to 57 of the 63 stock tickers initially earmarked for trading.

The objections, which have grown to 67 companies overall, include Apple, Nvidia, Microsoft, Amazon, Tesla, and Meta, threatening to strip the proposed blockchain-based marketplace of many of Wall Street's most widely traded names before its launch.

The setback comes just weeks after the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) introduced a regulatory exemption designed to accelerate tokenized stock trading, allowing qualifying platforms to offer blockchain-based shares through automated trading pools.

OKXICE, a joint venture equally owned by crypto exchange OKX's US holding company and Intercontinental Exchange, the parent of the New York Stock Exchange, plans to let investors trade tokenized versions of US stocks around the clock using stablecoins.

However, the growing corporate resistance has left the venture facing questions about which securities it can offer and whether investors will have sufficient liquidity to trade them.

The scale of the resistance has grown rapidly since OKXICE outlined its plans in early October.

By Oct. 30, 50 companies had registered objections. That figure rose to 62 by Nov. 4 before another five companies, including Meta, Palantir, S&P Global, KLA and Super Micro Computer, joined the list on Nov. 5.

Of the original 62 companies identified by OKXICE, 56 have now objected. The original proposal covered 63 tickers because Alphabet's two share classes were listed separately.

That leaves just six companies from the initial lineup without disclosed objections: Strategy, Coinbase, Robinhood, Circle, Securitize and BitGo.

All six have substantial connections to the cryptocurrency or digital asset industry, highlighting how far the venture's potential stock selection has shifted from the broader US equity market.

However, the absence of an objection does not establish that those companies have endorsed the proposal or that their tokenized shares will be available when trading begins.

The resistance is particularly significant because of how the SEC structured its September exemption.

Under the framework, companies whose shares are tokenized by unaffiliated third parties must receive written notice and an opportunity to object. A formal objection prevents the venue from offering those third-party tokenized shares under the exemption.

The framework leaves room for issuer-backed tokenization, meaning the objections do not necessarily represent opposition to blockchain-based securities in general.

Nevertheless, OKXICE has yet to announce a launch date or confirm how many stocks will ultimately be available. Its public website continues to advertise the trading venue as forthcoming.

Start with the stories that matter. Get the context behind the headlines and a clear view of what to watch next.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Even if OKXICE secures a viable selection of stocks, it faces another challenge: attracting enough capital to support trading.

The tokenization venture disclosed that neither it nor its affiliates will provide liquidity to its proposed marketplace.

Instead, eligible outside participants, including market makers and institutional investors, would need to deposit stock tokens and stablecoins into trading pools that facilitate transactions.

That model places considerable importance on the willingness of independent investors to commit capital, particularly outside traditional US market hours.

Existing tokenized equity markets offer a warning about the difficulties involved.

According to Dune's After Issuance report, the median decentralized trading pool for tokenized equities held just $92 as of Aug. 31.

Across 5,484 pools containing $69 million in assets, just 78 pools accounted for approximately $56 million, or 81% of the measured holdings. By protocol, Dune located $34 million on Uniswap, $17 million on PancakeSwap, and $11 million on Raydium.

This shows how concentrated liquidity remains across decentralized equity markets, despite growing interest in bringing traditional securities on-chain.

The $92 figure represents the median holdings of existing pools, rather than the liquidity available for every stock or a forecast of trading conditions on OKXICE.

The venture plans to use Uniswap v4-based pools on OKX's XLayer blockchain, where tokenized shares will trade against stablecoins through approved wallets.

Unlike conventional stock exchanges that match buyers and sellers through order books, the proposed system relies on assets deposited into pools to execute trades.

Insufficient capital in those pools could cause significant price slippage, particularly when investors try to execute large transactions or sell positions during market volatility.

That creates a significant challenge for a venture promising access to US equities beyond Wall Street's regular trading hours.

OKXICE must now overcome substantial issuer resistance while convincing outside market participants to finance trading in whatever tokenized stocks remain available.

Its eventual launch will provide the first meaningful test of whether the venue can assemble enough eligible securities and trading capital to deliver on its round-the-clock ambitions.

PS Plus Gets An Underwhelming Selection Of New Perks

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

PlayStation really needs to win back some goodwill with its user base right about now, and it seems one of the ways it plans on doing that is by trying to make PS Plus more appealing. A whole suite of new perks should do the trick, right? Well, maybe not.

The rest of the new perks are all pretty similar. A timed subscription to Calm, money off a DAZN sub, 5% off consoles and accessories, and 10% off games. It’s something, sure, and if PlayStation was in good standing with its users right now, it would probably be welcomed at best, or simply ignored at worst.

That’s not where PlayStation is right now, though. The company keeps making decisions that feel like it isn’t considering what its users want at all, so the slightly misleading reveal of these new perks has just generated even more fervor among PS5 owners.

The bulk of the replies to the PlayStation post are questioning how trials can be considered perks. Others have noted that most of the services for which PS Plus is offering trials actually offer free trials anyway, whether you have PS Plus or not.

You’d think money off a new console, at the very least, would be a welcome perk, but even that is being laughed at. It’s been highlighted that not only is a PS5 Pro still $855, even with the new 5% discount, but they’re not even in stock at the moment, so even if you do want one, you can’t have one.

The overall takeaway seems to be that this is a desperate, and poorly executed, attempt by PlayStation to get some of its users back on side. Like they have scraped together a few perks in the hope that bundling them together and adding them all at once might make people happy. If anything, it has done the exact opposite, and with console prices rising and the death of discs looming, PlayStation still doesn’t know how to get people to like it again, or perhaps it simply doesn’t care.

Huddersfield Town and Middlesbrough won’t believe what's happening at non-league side

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It's not uncommon for ex-EFL stars to wind down their careers in non-league, but what's happening at Harborough Town, especially, will catch the eye of Middlesbrough, Huddersfield Town and neutral fans, too.

Harborough are one of the fastest-rising non-league sides right now, having only just joined Step 6 in the non-league ladder in 2010. This season, they're competing in the National League North (Step 2) for the first time in their history, after winning the Southern League Premier Central last year.

With the likes of Morecambe, Macclesfield FC and Hereford now competing with them in league action, the need for extra quality and some ex-EFL professionals was needed in the summer, and continues to be needed as the year goes on.

And that led Mitch Austin's side to form quite the peculiar strike partnership.

Harborough hit headlines ahead of their first season at National League North level by securing the arrival of Britt Assombalonga.

The 33-year-old is still technically Middlesbrough's record signing, having arrived from Nottingham Forest for £15 million back in the summer of 2017. However, Will Lankshear will be set to break that once the add-ons in his move from Tottenham Hotspur are activated.

It's safe to say that Lankshear will probably be better remembered in a Boro shirt, but Assombalonga wasn't exactly a dud. He scored double figures in three of his four Championship campaigns at the Riverside, including 15 in his debut year to help his side reach the play-offs.

After leaving Teesside in 2021, the 33-year-old bounced around Turkish clubs, in between a short stint at Watford, before playing a small part in Barnet's season last year in League Two, and has now dropped a couple more divisions to play for Harborough.

Now, though, he has some competition up top in the form of Kyle Hudlin, the former Huddersfield man and widely recognised as the tallest outfield player in England, standing at six-foot-nine.

Football League World ranks all 24 Championship clubs by the longest-serving member of their squad.

Hudlin signed for Huddersfield off the back of scoring in the National League play-off final for Solihull Moors in 2022. He made just 10 appearances for the Terriers, spending the majority of his time out on loan, including a stint at Newport County, where he scored six in 27 League Two appearances in 2024/25.

After a year in Vietnam, he's returned to England and has signed for Harborough.

Harborough have been on quite the meteoric rise, with four promotions in 15 years, the final three of which have come in the last five seasons.

Their first in the National League North has been steady, with Mitch Austin's side sitting two points above the bottom four in what is turning out to be yet another very competitive Step 2 campaign, after last season saw two sides relegated on 50 or more points.

Assombalonga has been in and out of the side, owing to a few niggling injuries, but has two goals and two assists in six league starts this season.

Harborough do have a small excuse for a slower start than in previous years, though, as they've gone through three qualifying rounds in the FA Cup to reach the First Round proper for just the second time in their history, and for the second time in three years.

Those who remember them from two years ago will know that they took League One side Reading all the way at the Select Car Leasing Stadium in the second round, losing in extra time after drawing 3-3 with them after 90 minutes.

This season, they travel to another third-tier side in the first round, with Mitch Austin's men heading to Leyton Orient. They'll be wanting to cause another scare, and even follow through on upsetting the O's this weekend.

It's not only Assombalonga and Hudlin who have EFL reps under their belts in this Harborough side. Behind them is former three-time League One champion Michael Jacobs, who has over 450 appearances in the Football League to his name and was playing at Northampton Town last year.

It's those players who will be called upon by the Bees to help them through difficult matchups, not only in the league against ex-league sides, but also in FA Cup bouts against teams three divisions higher.

Do you think Harborough Town could cause an FA Cup upset this weekend? Let us know in the comments.

With the amount of ex-EFL scoring power in the squad now, there'll be an expectation for the club to start firing more in the National League North, of which their 13 goals are a divisional low.

As and when they do, Huddersfield and Middlesbrough fans may keep a small glancing eye on whether it's either of their former pro players doing so. And if they both combine against Leyton Orient on Saturday, it's sure to get some traction amongst footballing circles, too.

£300k Birmingham City man faces exit: Bolton Wanderers & Oxford United must react

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

As the winter months roll on, it won't be long until we're looking at how clubs across the EFL can improve their squads in the January transfer window.

Birmingham City will be looking to strengthen for what they hope will be a push towards securing a top-two finish and a place in the Premier League for the first time in 16 years.

But Chris Davies' side does have some deadwood that they'll need to rid themselves of, too, none more so than Scott Wright, who really hasn't settled at Birmingham at all since making the switch from Scottish giants Rangers in the summer of 2024 for around £300,000.

The 29-year-old has made just 24 appearances in the nearly two-and-a-half years he's spent at St Andrew's and was loaned out last January back to Scotland to see out the season with Dundee FC.

With just one one-minute cameo appearance in their EFL Cup to his name this season, he'll surely be angling for more game time, and Birmingham should be in a position to grant that.

Wright's career is a peculiar one. The former youth Scotland international was on the books at Rangers for two-and-a-half years, but never during his time at Ibrox was he a certified starter.

The winger was a very solid squad player during his time with the Gers, and a move south of the border for the first time in his career to a side expected to dominate League One in 2024/25 presented a good opportunity for him to take a more important role elsewhere.

That never came to fruition at Birmingham, though. Despite scoring a late winner on his debut against Wigan Athletic, injuries stopped Wright from playing much of a part in Chris Davies' side's record-breaking campaign.

Through AI, we'll rank the top 25 most intimidating football stadiums in British football, but which stadium comes out on top?

He played just 437 minutes of League One action, and a knee injury in just his third league start for the club in February 2025 not only curtailed his season, but also saw him out of the picture for the first half of last year's Championship return.

At that point, Birmingham had invested even more into the squad, and even though he did get off the bench for a few games back in January, it was always going to be a struggle to get into starting contention, especially as he wasn't while the Blues were in League One.

Wright needs a fresh start, and these two EFL sides should be on hand to give it to him.

Bolton Wanderers have had a fine start to life back in the Championship after a seven-year exile, sitting 17th with 17 points from 14 games and an away record which is amongst the best in the division.

Not many expected the Trotters to pack such a punch going forward, especially given the amount of quality they lost in those areas in the summer due to loanees returning to their parent club and the number of perceived top players they lost out on during the off-season.

Steven Schumacher has done well with the tools he's had, but it's evident that he still wants more, especially in the wide areas. Against Charlton Athletic on Tuesday night, he opted for Max Lott on the left wing, a 19-year-old they signed from Aston Villa's academy in October, for his first senior start for the club.

Five players, five careers. Name them from the clubs they played for.

Each day there are five current or former EFL players to identify. For each one you’ll see their senior clubs in the order they played for them. Loan spells are left out.

You get four guesses per player. Every wrong guess unlocks a clue: first their position, then their nationality, then their initials.

Get it first time for 4 points, then 3, 2 and 1. The best possible score is 20. A new set of players arrives at midnight.

Some saw that as a potential cry for some reinforcements in January. Thierry Gale aside, the Trotters don't look stunning on the wings. Sam Illing-Junior hasn't excelled, and Kyliane Dong hasn't done much to nail down a starting spot.

Additionally, both of those wingers are on loan at Bolton, so they won't be theirs in the future.

If Bolton can agree on a minimal deal to bring Wright to the Toughsheet Stadium, they'll have a permanent player entering their prime and with a big point to prove that he's good enough to play regular Championship games after not getting a sniff at Birmingham.

The likelihood is, though, that if Wright does remain in the EFL in January, he may find himself dropping down to the top of League One.

If Oxford United's transfer embargo, which forbade them from investing in Aaron Ramsey's squad this past summer, is lifted by January, as the higher-ups at the Kassam Stadium are confident it will be, they should have an eye on Wright to increase their attacking depth.

The Yellows will be in for a busy winter window if the embargo is lifted. Oxford have started the campaign brilliantly under Ramsey, who has taken to his first senior managerial role very well. After their win over Reading on Wednesday, they sit third in League One, three points off the top and with a game in hand.

Where do you think Scott Wright will end up in January? Let us know in the comments.

However, there are some worries about the lack of senior depth in the squad. Ramsey has been brilliant at utilising players from the academy, and it's led to 18-year-old Mo Missanga being one of the more exciting stars in the division.

But if they can bring in some experience out wide, that would be optimal, especially with Villa loanee Luka Lynch only at the club until January.

Wright would automatically be one of the more veteran attacking players in the Oxford side if they moved for him, and he could provide a good boost, if given the right minutes, as the Yellows look to maintain promotion form.

Amazon Fire TV Stick HD Review: Small, speedy, and easy on the wallet

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Amazon Fire TV Stick HD is a simple, affordable way to make a 1080p TV smarter. The new Vega OS feels quick, its slimmer design can run straight off your TV's USB port, and setup is a breeze. It's limited to 1080p and can't sideload apps like Fire OS, so if you own a 4K TV, you should spend a bit more on the new Amazon Fire Stick 4K.

Responsive, easy-to-use interface with new Vega OS

Slim design that runs off your TV's USB port

No third-party app sideloading like Fire OS

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

Streaming sticks don't need to be fancy to hit their mark, and the Amazon Fire TV Stick HD is a great reminder of that. If you've got an older TV that's still going strong but has sluggish smart features (or perhaps none at all), a $35 streaming stick is an easy fix.

The 2026 Fire TV Stick HD runs Vega OS, Amazon's Linux-based software, which replaces the Android-based Fire OS that powered previous models. Amazon says the changes make it more than 30 percent faster, and it also brings a slimmer body, USB power, Wi-Fi 6 support, and free Alexa+.

It's not all pros across the board. The resolution caps out at 1080p, you can't sideload third-party apps anymore, and there's no support for Dolby Vision. But if you're shopping for a bedroom, guest room, or an older TV set that you don't primarily use, the Fire TV Stick HD is one of the cheaper streaming devices you should take a closer look at.

The Amazon Fire TV Stick HD launched on April 29, 2026, for $34.99, which is the same price as the 2024 model it replaced. It's Amazon's cheapest streaming stick, and it sits below the Fire TV Stick 4K and the Fire TV Stick 4K Max. Amazon ran a limited-time deal that brought the stick down to $21.99 shortly after launch, so we'll likely see temporary price cuts around the holiday shopping season.

Keep in mind that the stick's new "Direct Power" feature means that it gets power right from your TV's USB port. If your TV doesn't have a USB port, you'll need to pick up an adapter separately.

The Fire TV Stick HD is about as minimal as a streaming device gets, and Amazon says it's the slimmest Fire TV device the company has made. That makes it really easy to tuck behind a TV or toss in a bag for a trip, and it won't crowd the other ports and cables on your set.

The only other connection is a USB-C port for power, and thanks to "Direct Power," you can plug it straight into your TV's USB port with the included cable. That means you'll have one less wall adapter to hide, and your setup will look a lot cleaner as a result.

Sign up to the weekly Tom's Streaming Guide to stay in the loop with the latest releases and top recommendations across your favorite streaming services. We’ve got you covered with a complete guide on what’s worth watching.

The included Alexa Voice Remote covers all the basics, including voice search, menu navigation, TV power, and volume controls. It doesn't have fancy extras like a remote finder or backlit buttons, but it gets the job done. (If you want Amazon's redesigned Fire TV remote, which I liked a lot more, you'll have to get the Fire TV Stick 4K.)

Setup is about as easy as it gets. Just plug the stick into your TV's HDMI port, connect the USB-C cable to your TV's USB port, and follow the on-screen prompts. You'll then have to connect to Wi-Fi and sign in with your Amazon account, and you're off to the races.

Since Vega OS only lets you install apps from the Amazon app store, you should make sure that the ones you use most are actually available. Netflix, YouTube, Disney+, Prime Video, Hulu, and other popular streaming services all run natively, but niche apps may be missing due to the lack of third-party app sideloading, which the older Fire OS allowed you to do.

The Fire TV Stick HD has a quad-core 1.7GHz processor and 8GB of storage. It can stream up to 1080p resolution with HDR10, HDR10+, and HLG, and it supports Dolby Atmos through HDMI pass-through if you have a compatible sound setup. There's no Dolby Vision here, and it won't output 4K, so it's really only meant for 1080p TV sets.

Vega OS keeps menus and home screen rows moving smoothly, and apps open quickly. I didn't have any issues switching between my most used apps on the fly. Amazon's "30 percent faster" figure is its own claim, so I wouldn't treat it as fact, but it definitely felt faster than my past Fire OS experiences.

I also like that the Fire TV Stick HD isn't limited to video. You can pair a Bluetooth game controller and play cloud games through Amazon Luna or Xbox Game Pass, which is a fun bonus, and you can even connect a pair of headphones for late-night viewing. It comes with Alexa+ free of charge, which can come in handy when you're trying to hunt down something new to watch.

The downsides are pretty familiar to past Fire TV Sticks. A big chunk of the Fire TV home screen is taken up by recommendations and sponsored content, and you'll definitely notice it (especially if you're used to Roku's cleaner layout like I am).

Sideloading third-party apps is also gone, which will bug those who liked using older Fire TV Sticks for apps that aren't available in Amazon's app store. Neither is a dealbreaker for the average viewer, but they're worth knowing about before diving in.

The Amazon Fire TV Stick HD is a fast, simple, and affordable streaming stick that's a perfect match for older HD TVs and secondary rooms. Vega OS feels speedy, the slim design and USB power keep things neat, and setup takes just a few minutes. You won't get 4K streaming resolution, Dolby Vision, or third-party app sideloading, but for $34.99 (or less on sale), it's hard to complain.

If you're looking for an alternative pick, the base model Roku Streaming Stick ($39) also tops out at 1080p, but has a cleaner interface.

In Amazon’s ecosystem, the new Fire TV Stick 4K costs $25 more, but you get 4K resolution output and a redesigned (and much better) remote. And if you're a tinkerer who wants to sideload apps, the Fire TV Stick 4K Max is the only Amazon stick that still runs Android-based Fire OS.

If you've got an older 1080p TV and want a cheap way to get streaming apps on it, the Fire TV Stick HD is a solid choice that won't break the bank.

Dylan Haas is a Freelance Contributor and Lab Tester for Tom’s Guide. He’s a seasoned journalist with bylines at publications like Mashable, GameSpot, and Paste Magazine where he covered the latest in tech, gaming, and entertainment.

Thailand SEC Files Criminal Complaint Against Bitget Ahead of Dec. 5 Access Block

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Thailand's SEC filed a criminal complaint against Bitget operator BG Limited for unlicensed Thai trading from 2022 to 2025.

Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission filed a criminal complaint Thursday against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, alleging it ran an unlicensed digital-asset exchange for Thai users from 2022 to 2025, and notified the government to block the platform from Dec. 5.

The complaint, lodged with the Economic Crime Suppression Division, also names Mr. Kritsana Kritsananuwat as an alleged supporter who promoted Bitget through Facebook and YouTube channels, the SEC said in statement No. 241/2026.

From 2022 through 2025, the regulator found, Bitget offered a trading system in the ordinary course of business, charged fees, ran a Thai-language website that could show values in baht, and solicited Thai users on social channels including “Bitget Thailand” on X, “BGB Thai Holder” on Telegram and “bitgetthailand” on TikTok.

That conduct amounts to operating a digital-asset exchange under Section 3 of the Emergency Decree on Digital Asset Businesses B.E. 2561 (2018) without a license, a violation of Section 26 punishable under Section 66, the SEC said. The case now moves to an inquiry official, then prosecutors and the courts.

Separately, the SEC submitted Bitget’s website, other domains and its app to the Ministry of Digital Economy and Society under a 2025 royal decree that authorizes access blocks on unlicensed trading platforms. From Dec. 5, the public “will be unable to access the platform,” the statement said, and investors still holding assets there were told to consider acting before the cutoff.

The enforcement push lands weeks after Bitget announced a planned acquisition of GMO-Z.com Cryptonomics (Thailand), a Bangkok digital-asset exchange and brokerage that already holds Thai licenses, through its interest in BTGT Management (Thailand) Ltd.

Under that deal, GMO Internet and its Thai holding company would transfer their entire stake in GMO-Z — about 49% and 50.94%, respectively — to BTGT or a designee at completion, a Sept. 24 GMO Internet filing showed. Bitget said completion remains subject to regulatory approval and, after a change-of-control nod, it intends to introduce its trading platform to Thai users under local rules.

“Thailand is an important part of Southeast Asia’s digital asset landscape, and this acquisition gives us a great opportunity to establish local operations,” Bitget CEO Gracy Chen said in the Oct. 20 release.

The timetable has already slipped. GMO Internet Group pushed the effective date of the share transfer from Oct. 31 to Nov. 30, citing careful work on Thai regulatory applications and closing procedures. That revised date sits five days before the planned Bitget access block.

Thailand has used the same access-blocking tool before. In mid-2025 the SEC referred Bybit, OKX and three other unlicensed platforms for a June 28 cutoff, telling users to move assets first. The Bitget action follows that template — and adds a criminal complaint against the exchange operator and a named promoter.

Licensed venues still sit at the center of Bangkok’s policy mix. Thailand has approved a multi-year capital-gains tax break only for trades on SEC-regulated platforms, and has been expanding regulated crypto products in derivatives and capital markets. Unlicensed platforms get the opposite treatment: investor alerts, access blocks and, now for Bitget, a police referral.

What is not yet clear is whether a criminal case against BG Limited for the 2022–2025 period bars, slows or leaves untouched BTGT’s bid to take over a licensed local operator. The SEC statement does not mention GMO-Z. Bitget has not publicly answered the complaint in a statement posted with the acquisition release.

For Thai users still on Bitget, the practical clock is Dec. 5. For Bitget, the test is whether Nov. 30 closes a regulated door into Thailand — or whether the complaint filed Thursday makes that licensed path the next question the ECD and the SEC have to answer.

I’ve reviewed almost every Android foldable this year — here’s how the iPhone Duo compares

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Apple’s first foldable matches its Android rivals in a few key areas, but it falls short in others.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

When I first read Allison and Nilay’s Verge review of the iPhone Duo, I was immediately struck by one of their complaints: that the cover display is too small. That’s not how the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8 feels, I thought. Had they got it wrong?

After a week and a half using the Duo, I know exactly what they mean — and it’s not the screen’s fault at all. Apps that feel comfortable on Samsung’s short foldable and the similarly sized Xiaomi 18 Fold are often cramped and compact on the Duo’s cover screen, robbed of screen space by a right-hand rail that most apps don’t make use of anyway.

That’s just one of the ways that Apple’s first foldable feels fundamentally different from the Android alternatives. I’ve reviewed almost every Android foldable that was released this year, from Samsung and Google to Honor and Oppo, and I’ve been surprised to realize that the Duo holds its own against them all on hardware, but is let down by iOS itself. For now at least, Android is slicker for folding phones — though I don’t know how long that will last.

The iPhone Duo feels great. Mostly. Its curved frame and Ceramic Shield back have that ineffable Apple feeling, a sense of polish that helps justify the $2,000 price tag. The hinge in particular is satisfyingly smooth, closing and opening with a comforting magnetic click. Compared with the Galaxy Z Fold 8 or Xiaomi 18 Fold, the Duo just feels better.

But part of that is down to the Duo’s big drawback: its weight. At first it feels encouraging, the reassuring heft of all that glass and metal. Then I started using the phone, and I realized it’s just plain heavy. It weighs more than all of its rivals — even ones with larger screens — and any attempt to use the Duo one-handed makes that obvious quickly. It’s a little thicker than some other foldables, too, though in practice that bothers me far less.

The squat shape won’t be for everyone. The Duo is even shorter and wider than the Z Fold 8, though almost identical in dimensions to Xiaomi’s 18 Fold. I’m a fan of this design, which is both cute and comfortable when folded shut and gives you a natural choice of portrait or landscape orientations when open. The major downsides are that the short cover screen makes some apps awkward to use, and it’s wide when you need to stretch across to the other side — a problem worsened by some of Apple’s software choices. More on that later.

And while the Duo’s IP68 rating is an obvious advantage over much of the competition (only Google and Honor can match it), we’ve not seen many recent Android foldables struggle with hinge and display damage quite like the Duo. It’s still not clear how widespread Duo screen failures really are — they don’t seem to be as ubiquitous as Samsung’s first-generation failures back in 2019 — but non-impact damage like this is almost unheard of in modern Android foldables. It’s a reminder that the Duo is still a first-gen product while some Android rivals are into their eighth iteration.

The screen is the Duo’s smartest hardware trick, and easily my favorite thing about the phone compared with other foldable phones. No rival offers anything like the “nano-texture” (read: matte) finish on the inner screen, which is easy on the eyes in more ways than one.

Crucially, it also helps mask the phone’s crease, which isn’t quite invisible but is well-hidden. The Duo’s crease is somewhere in the middle of the Android pack: subtler than Google’s and Honor’s, more obvious than Samsung’s. Oppo’s near-invisible crease remains the best, but not by much. The Duo’s matte screen means it’s harder than most to see (though visible from an angle), but it’s still easy enough to feel, and a couple of friends have immediately remarked on it when trying the phone. Still, I don’t want to over-egg it: The Pixel 11 Pro Fold is probably the only one out of this year’s foldables with a crease I’d say is a problem. The Duo’s crease is easy to ignore.

I’d actually say the Duo’s under-display camera is more of an annoyance. It’s not only inside an extra-pixelated circle of screen, but that’s the only patch without the matte coating, which makes it a little more distracting. I can mostly tune it out, but not entirely. I’d probably prefer a regular selfie camera tucked right into the corner, as on the Xiaomi 18 Fold and a few others, but I suspect opinions will differ here.

Finally, the Duo benefits from stylus support, letting you use a USB-C Apple Pencil on the central screen. This isn’t unique — Samsung used to offer the same, and both of Honor’s and Oppo’s latest foldables work with their styli — but plenty of the current Android crop don’t offer the option. I don’t have an Apple Pencil, so I haven’t been able to test it myself, but my colleague Allison is clearly a fan.

The iPhone Duo’s cameras, like those on most foldables, are fairly basic, with a pair of 48-megapixel rear lenses, one main and one ultrawide. It’s a setup comparable to the Z Fold 8, and both phones’ cameras are good, but not great. Most other Android foldables (including Samsung’s Z Fold 8 Ultra) instead offer triple rear cameras, netting you a big boost in photographic flexibility. They often use larger sensors than either Apple or Samsung, so their main cameras are better, too. Still, Vivo is the only manufacturer getting close to slab flagship quality in its foldable cameras, and its hardware lags behind in a few other respects that would prevent it from being my foldable recommendation overall.

As for selfies, the front-screen camera is pretty basic, but you can take better shots using the rear cameras by opening the phone up and flipping it round. That under-display camera is worse than a regular selfie camera, but good enough so long as you only use it for video calls.

As for battery, Nilay and Allison had different experiences in our Duo review. I’ve mostly found the battery long-lasting, easily running a full day of mixed use. It’s got a higher capacity than either of Samsung’s foldables, so that’s no real surprise. It still pales in comparison to the two-day battery of Honor’s Magic V6, but once you’re past lasting a day, I think you get diminishing returns anyway.

So far, the Duo looks like a competitive foldable, albeit a little middle-of-the-pack. It beats its rivals in some hardware areas, falls behind in others, and has one truly unique selling point in the matte foldable display.

Unusually for Apple, the software is what lets it down.

The biggest problem isn’t Apple’s own software, but everyone else’s: Most apps still aren’t optimized for the Duo. In the best case, that means apps that don’t make full use of the Duo’s new right-hand rail, showing an empty black bar instead of app controls. Worse are the ones that don’t even scale to the larger screen — apps like Bluesky or Duolingo can use the whole display on other foldables, but appear in thin strips with huge black bars on the Duo.

This will improve with time. I’ve seen Google Maps, Google Translate, and Instagram update in the last few days alone, and no doubt more will follow. Some are already better on the Duo: Slack uses a helpful desktop-style two-column layout, while on Android foldables you just get the mobile UI but bigger. But for now at least, most apps run better on my Android foldables than on my Duo.

OS-level improvements are harder to predict. At first I loved the idea of Apple moving key controls to the right-hand edge, but it takes up so much space that it leaves the Duo’s cover screen feeling smaller than it needs to: You get all the awkward stretching of a wide phone without the expansive screen space. Samsung and Xiaomi’s wide foldables may be about the same size, but in use they feel much bigger. I wonder how many software developers will follow Google’s lead in app optimization — it essentially ignored the right rail entirely while updating Google Maps, instead just spreading the map to the edge of the screen. It feels bigger and better than most other Duo apps as a result.

And this is partly the Android user in me, but the lack of a persistent back-swipe gesture seems to grate more on the Duo than on other iPhones. Sometimes, moving backward in an app requires a button in the right rail, sometimes one in the top-left corner, sometimes a swipe from the left-hand edge of the screen. It’s annoyingly inconsistent, and more often than not requires stretching across the phone’s width, whether it’s open or closed. Being able to swipe back from either side of the screen gives Android foldables a huge advantage in one-handed use.

As for multitasking, the Duo is well behind the competition. You can run two apps at once in split-screen, but only in landscape mode, and each must take up exactly half the display. Compare this to Oppo, which allows you to fluidly move between multiple split-screen apps and floating windows; or Xiaomi, which allows up to six apps to run side by side in split-screen, and Apple’s implementation feels downright basic. These are the extreme end of Android, and probably overkill for most (they certainly are for me), but I’d take them over Apple’s half-hearted multitasking any day.

Apple hasn’t waded into the foldable market and fixed all its flaws. If anything, “crackgate” is proof that it’s still playing catch-up with the Android alternatives, which have a few years of fine-tuning behind them. Many are still lighter, and thinner, and have subtler creases than the Duo, or bigger batteries and better cameras. In broad strokes, though, this hardware is up to scratch — and, crucially, on par with its most direct worldwide rival, the Galaxy Z Fold 8.

For now, though, Android has the software edge. Google has worked hard to make its OS adaptable to various screen sizes so most apps take full advantage of the bigger inner screen while individual OEMs have designed ambitious multitasking tools. That isn’t yet true for iOS, and some of Apple’s own software tweaks in the OS make things more awkward still. It’s those that I’m worried about long term. The best thing about foldables is their flexibility — something Apple itself often lacks. If the Duo is going to catch up to Android, Apple might have to adapt.

Square Enix accidentally botches a Nier: Automata update, balloons required file size from 50GB to 366GB on PC

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

"Do you think games are silly little things?"

Yesterday, Nier: Automata received a new patch on Steam, adding more language support to the title - it can now be played with Korean, Simplified Chinese, and Traditional Chinese options - but this new update also, somehow, inflated the file size of the game on PC from about 50GB to 366GB. D'oh! What's worse is, the update is not optional.

The cause of this, reportedly, is a bug that caused all the DLC in the YoRHa Edition of the game to become the same size as the base game - and the new download stacks them all concurrently. "Only SquareEnix can turn 40GB into 366GB with 8GB patch," says one dissatisfied Steam reviewer.

If you don't have space for this massive new file on your PC (and I can't say I blame you, it's ridiculous), there is a workaround, as confirmed by Square Enix in a tweet via its Japanese Twitter account.

"We have confirmed an issue where, following the latest update to the Steam version of Nier: Automata, the installation process indicates that more storage space is required than expected," noted the publisher. "We are currently investigating the cause and working on a solution. We sincerely apologize for the inconvenience. As a temporary workaround, you can download the base game by unchecking the DLC under Steam → Properties → DLC."

It's quite ironic, really, that so many Steam reviewers are complaining about things like "I just need to delete 90% of my computer to install this" when a large part of the game is managing the software/programmes installed on your android protagonists in order to upgrade your abilities and stats. There's even an ending for uninstalling your OS on your mechanical main characters. I'd be inclined to suggest this is some sort of meta psy-op from creator Yoko Taro, but… nah, that's just fantasy, isn't it? Isn't it?

Speaking of Taro, earlier this year we learned that "Nier Automata will continue", though we only heard back in 2025 that most of the projects the developer was working on were cancelled. One of these may have been the Tencent-backed Nier mobile game in 2024, which reportedly "struggled to find a monetisation to offset its high costs."

Paraguay Bank Collapse Shows Why Bitcoin Was Created as USDT Hits Premium

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Paraguay bank crisis is showing why Bitcoin was created in the first place. USDT sellers reportedly demanded premiums of up to 13% after authorities placed major digital lender Ueno Bank into resolution.

MILLIONS of people LOCKED OUT of their own money as Ueno bank COLLAPSES IN Paraguay President Pena faces IMPEACHMENT — reports pic.twitter.com/3vb8z7MIQm

The crisis left 3 million customers temporarily unable to access their accounts. Some peer-to-peer (P2P) listings showed Tether’s USDT trading around 6,942 Paraguayan guaraníes. That compared with the central bank’s November 4 dollar reference of 6,144.02 guaraníes.

The USDT premium shows growing demand for dollar-denominated assets outside conventional banking. It also revives a question that shaped Bitcoin’s creation: what happens when people cannot freely access money held inside financial institutions?

The Central Bank of Paraguay initiated Ueno Bank’s resolution process on November 3. Regulators found its solvency ratio had fallen below half the legally required minimum following adjustments to its financial statements. Ueno itself had grown rapidly since launching in 2021, becoming one of Paraguay’s most prominent digital lenders.

The bank claimed 3 million customers and managed around $850 million in public funds. Following the intervention, the bank temporarily restricted customer access to accounts and disrupted financial services. Authorities began arranging account transfers and deposit protection while investigators examined the bank’s operations.

The crisis also triggered political fallout. President Santiago Peña announced his resignation on November 5 following mounting pressure over Ueno’s collapse. Reuters reported that prosecutors were investigating him, while authorities issued arrest warrants for eight bank executives.

Meanwhile, the USDT premium attracted attention across cryptocurrency markets. A November 5 review by Criptoinforme examined 80 Binance P2P advertisements. It found that the median price across the 10 best offers for buying USDT stood near 6,399 guaraníes. This represented a 3.4% premium over exchange-house dollar prices.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

Paraguay’s banking problems carry an interesting historical connection to Bitcoin’s creation. Satoshi Nakamoto published the Bitcoin whitepaper on October 31, 2008, during the global financial crisis. It proposed a peer-to-peer electronic cash system that could transfer value without relying on financial institutions.

The message became even clearer when Nakamoto mined Bitcoin’s genesis block on January 3, 2009. Its embedded newspaper headline read: “Chancellor on brink of second bailout for banks.”

Bitcoin was designed to address the limitations of a financial system built around trusted intermediaries. Its decentralized network allows users to hold and transfer BTC without requiring permission from a conventional bank.

Paraguay’s situation demonstrates why that proposition still matters. If a bank restricts access to customer accounts, financial independence becomes more than an abstract crypto talking point.

Bitcoin takes another approach through decentralized ownership and self-custody. Users who control their private keys can access their BTC without depending on a bank to authorize withdrawals.

Bitcoin’s original promise was never simply about making investors wealthier. It was about giving people another way to hold and transfer value without depending entirely on the banking system. Paraguay has provided a timely reminder of why that idea emerged in the first place.

How long West Brom face without George Campbell as James Morrison confirms injury extent

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

West Brom host Millwall in the Championship this Saturday, and they will hope to extend their league unbeaten run to six games. However, WBA will have to do so without club captain George Campbell, who withdrew from the Southampton draw after feeling his groin.

Now, ahead of the Millwall tie, Baggies boss James Morrison has confirmed Campbell will be absent against the Lions. Speaking to the club’s official media, he said: “Unfortunately George picked up a groin injury against Southampton and we expect him to be out for between ten to 12 days. It’s not ideal, but it could’ve been worse. Hopefully it’ll just be one game that he misses.”

Campbell has played 15 times in all competitions this season, sitting unused on the bench on only one occasion. As captain, he serves as a key presence at centre-back, so while it is a blow to lose him, there will be relief that he isn’t out for an extended spell.

There’s no hiding that Campbell is a loss for Albion. He was instrumental in their survival turnaround last season and emerged as a real leader, earning the captain’s armband and retaining a key role for the 2026/27 campaign.

Thankfully for West Brom and Morrison, he has a straightforward selection decision when dealing with Campbell’s absence. Nat Phillips replaced him at the break on Tuesday night, and he will more than likely do so from the start against Millwall.

In the two Championship games Campbell has not started, it has been fellow centre-back Chris Mepham who has stepped in as captain. As such, he seems the likely candidate to do so against the Lions, although Jayson Molumby has donned the armband before as well.

Morrison will be hoping his remaining WBA centre-backs can avoid suspension or injury, as only Mepham and Phillips remain with Campbell sidelined.

Campbell was called up for the United States national team for their November fixtures. But, with the extent of his injury now confirmed, it will be expected that he withdraws.

That will give the West Brom man the break to recover from his groin issue and work back to fitness for the return to club football. WBA face Swansea City in the first game back, and the hope is that Campbell will be fit.

Morrison and the physios will be sure to be careful with Campbell to ensure he doesn’t suffer a further setback. Losing him for one game is a blow, but seeing him cast to the sidelines for longer would spark real concern given the Baggies’ lack of centre-back depth.

These Tulips Look Just Like Peonies – and Their Ravishing Flowers Last for Months, Not Days

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →
