'Carnage' - Ex-Wolves goalkeeper Carl Ikeme offers insight into Doncaster Rovers' infamous experiment

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Former Doncaster Rovers goalkeeper Carl Ikeme has lifed the lid on his experience of the club’s now-infamous 2011/12 season ‘experiment’.

The now-retired stopper had two loan spells at Doncaster during that ill-fated Championship campaign, building experience away from his parent club Wolverhampton Wanderers.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

He was dropped into an environment far from ordinary, as Doncaster turned to high-profile figures signed to short-term deals to keep them in the second tier.

Among those recruited with the help of agent Willie McKay were former Liverpool maverick El Hadji Diouf, ex-Newcastle United defender Habib Beye and France-capped frontman Frédéric Piquionne.

“It was a bit chaotic in all honesty,” Ikeme told The Yorkshire Post. “I'm sure a lot of people have said the same.

“We had El Hadji Diouf and Pascal Chimbonda and a few other characters. I think there was talent in the squad, but I think in order to get a few of these players here, Dean Saunders kind of had to let them do what they wanted in a way, which didn't really help Doncaster.

“It was frustrating for a lot of the players that were training all the time. You probably heard some lads would just come on a Friday and train and play Saturday.

“It all seemed okay at the time if you're looking maybe from an outside point of view, but when you're in it yourself, it's a bit of a p**s take.”

Ikeme only dropped in at Doncaster for two short-term loans but committed to relocating to the area from his Midlands home.

“It was frustrating because I was living up there and obviously you had lads who would come down on a Friday,” the now-40-year-old said. “I'm staying up Doncaster all week, which is fine because that's my job.

“But it just gets frustrating I think and you had some good lads there as well, but it was hard work in all honesty.”

The so-called experiment was unequivocally a failure and Doncaster tumbled into League One at the end of the season.

Ikeme was back at Wolves by the time relegation was confirmed and did not leave Molineux again, seeing out his career with the club before retiring in 2018.

“It makes you know what you're not about, and what you don't want,” he said. “But at the same time I met some great people at Doncaster.

“Dave ‘Mud’ Richards, the kitman, great guy. Mal Purchase, who went everywhere with Dean, another great character man, really good guy. The goalkeeper coach, there were a lot of good people there behind the scenes, but it was just carnage and it wasn't how a team should really be run.

“I understand it to a degree, but football is a tough game and sometimes it doesn't really matter if you've got players that are supposed to be above the level, if they don't really care then it's going to be difficult.

“Obviously the Championship's outrageously hard anyway, and if everyone's not pulling in the same direction it becomes really difficult, and that was kind of a moment where it was really difficult.

“I liked to play games, which was good. The club, really good club as well. I really had a lot of time for Doncaster and everyone up there, it was just the situation at the time that wasn't ideal.”

Ikeme spoke The Yorkshire Post on behalf of Midnite. Midnite, the Principal Partner of Wolverhampton Wanderers for the 2026/27 season, are providing luxury coach travel on the ‘Midnite Express’ for 100 Wolverhampton Wanderers supporters, as they set off on a 130-mile-round trip to watch their beloved Wolves take on the league’s glamour club, Wrexham, this Saturday, November 7.

Fans will be joined on the Express by Wolves club legends, Steve Bull, Carl Ikeme and Matt Jarvis and treated to club-centric quizzes and live Q&As, with exclusive prizes up for grabs.

My AT Thru-Hike: By the Numbers

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Hey folks, back at it with another post here.  I’ve been meaning to write a recap of my hike pretty much sense I finished hiking the trail back in August, but alas, I am only getting to it now. Now that I am settled firmly into post-trail life, back home and working again, I took the chance to analyze my hike a bit. Compiled below are some interesting or funny insights (at least to me) of my thru-hike of the Appalachian Trail.  Hopefully you will find this insightful if you are planning your own AT thru-hike or at least entertaining…

Here is a complete breakdown of the costs that I incurred while on my At thru-hike.  While I don’t think I was egregious in my spending, I didn’t exactly skimp either.  I could have easily shaved off some expenses, especially in the food department.  However, after long hiking days, I simply enjoyed the comforts provided by cold beverages and warm food!

Right out of the pouch, just like God intended!

Along the trail I saw and experienced much, including wildlife!  The highlight here were the Fishers!  I only got a quick glimpse of both of them, as they are quite elusive creatures, but I will take what I can get!  And while I saw quite a few deer all along the trail, the deer in Shenandoah and the Smokies were shameless!  Often coming right up to my tent at night or in the morning, including one that didn’t even flinch when I popped my head out and was close enough I could have pet it!

This was surprisingly close to Tinker Cliffs in the Virginia Triple Crown area!

For sleeping along the trail I definitely preferred my tent over shelters, and spent a good deal of the trail actively avoiding shelters!  In Maine I softened this stance quite a bit for a few reasons.  The first being that Maine was the rainiest state of my hike, so there was some shelter hopping from one to another in the attempt to keep some of my gear and myself dry.  The second being I was exhausted a lot towards the end and saving a little bit of time and energy by sleeping in the shelter was worth it.  And finally, towards the end I just couldn’t bother to care about mice running around me at night that much.  Plus, the rest of the Board (Shooter and Birdie) was doing it!

First night on trail at Hidden Pond during the March Megastorm. I met friends that I would keep through the end of the trail. Especially Shooter, Queso, Zipper, and Beer Run!

Here are some fun and random stats from my hike.  These add some color to the story of my hike. Like falling 10ft off a dam, cutting your leg open, and needing to get stitches!

Day 11 – Pulling the Ripcord

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

After a long night of contemplation and hard choices, I decided to get off the trail.  I considered continuing to Tan Hill and its famous pub.  However, I believe that Tan Hill is even more remote than Keld.  If I arrived and decided that I needed medical assistance, it would be nearly impossible to arrange.  Additionally, the pub at Tan Hill was completely booked, so I would have to rely on camping on the grounds around the pub.

I would need to find my way to the nearest town and make a plan from there.  Somehow, I still need to get to Kirk Yetholm by my previously-planned arrival date.  I mailed my extra gear to the Border Hotel and I have a two-night reservation.  Graeme and Rachel told me that they will be finishing the Way on that same day.  If I keep to my schedule, I could meet up with them at the end of the Pennine and celebrate their accomplishment.

Two days after that, I need to be in Edinburgh for my scheduled flight home.

After packing up my camp, I walked into the Keld proper.  It was even smaller and more remote than I realized.  Sitting at the Keld Green Café, sipping a cappuccino, I discovered that the nearest town was Kirkby Stephan, about 10 miles away.  There are no buses in Keld.  I’d be looking at a 4 hour walk at best.

I tried using the Uber app, but after an hour of searching, the app literally apologized and gave up.  I tried a taxi app as well and received the same result.  No one was willing to make the drive from Kirkby Stephen.  I talked to the café owner; he suggested I try hitchhiking.

As I sat contemplating my options, I met Richard and Jackie, two people who were walking England’s Coast-to-Coast trail.  That trail intersects the Pennine Way at Keld.  Jackie was not taking my situation lightly.  She got on her phone and started calling taxi companies in Kirkby Stephen.  Before long, she had arranged to have a taxi make the long haul to Keld and back.  It was pricey, but at this point, I was glad to pay it.

It was almost an hour before the cab showed up.  While I waited, Jackie insisted on buying me another cappuccino.  When the driver pulled up, I thanked her profusely, declared to everyone at the café that she was a living angel, and ran for the cab.

On the drive down to Kirkby Stephan, I could see why drivers were reluctant to pick up fares from Keld.  The country road is long, narrow, and winding.  I’m not prone to motion sickness, but this road really pushed my tolerance.  After 40 minutes, we pulled up in front of the Pennine Inn.  I tipped the driver generously and went inside.  The room was lovely, and the staff, even lovelier.  Realizing that I was injured, they went out of their way to help me.  They were absolutely incredible.

Now came the task of figuring out how I was going to get from this inn to Kirk Yetholm in Scotland, nearly 120 miles away.  After hours on my phone, planning, purchasing train tickets, and making hotel reservations, I came up with the following itinerary:

I made all my reservations at hotels and inns within walking distance of the nearest train station.  Many of them were across the street from the station.

The next issue was timing the day of arrival in Kirk Yetholm.  My impression was that this tiny village would not offer a lot of entertainment or distraction.  Getting there too early was not going to be much fun.  Therefore, I decided that I would take a day or two at the end of each travel leg on my schedule.  Be a tourist!  See some of the sites along my route and do my best to enjoy my time in the UK.

And that’s what I did.  I visited towns and cities, toured castles, ate good food, touched the North Sea, and generally just wandered around admiring whatever crossed my path.  Unfortunately, I was still limping, even with the ankle brace, and had to limit my walking distance.  There were occasional bouts of melancholy over my failed attempt at the Pennine.  I felt good about walking nearly 120 miles of the Way, certainly the longest distance I had ever experienced by a very wide margin.  Mostly, I just tried to enjoy my travels.

The Airlines Offering Premium Economy On Airbus A380 Flights

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Brandon's passion for aviation started at a young age. He became involved in the flight simulator and VATSIM communities, which sparked his interest in aviation and eventually led him to earn his private pilot license. Outside of work, he continues to build flight hours, explore new airports, and stay involved in the aviation community.

Six of the 10 airlines still flying the Airbus A380 offer a premium economy cabin on the type. British Airways will shortly have the largest at 84 seats, following the retrofit announced on October 5, 2026, which also gives its aircraft the highest premium ratio of any A380 at roughly 48%. All Nippon Airways (ANA) follows at 73 seats, then Qantas at 60, Emirates at 56, Lufthansa at 52, and Singapore Airlines at 44.

The four without it are Qatar Airways, Etihad, Korean Air and Asiana. Qatar's layout of 8 first, 48 business and 461 economy produces a premium ratio of around 11%, the lowest among A380 operators. Where the cabin exists, the experience varies considerably. Emirates won the Skytrax award for best premium economy seat in 2026 and is adding the cabin to 68 A380 routes. ANA fits 73 premium economy seats against only 56 in business and flies them on a single route between Tokyo Narita International Airport (NRT) and Honolulu (HNL).

British Airways announced a full retrofit of its Airbus A380 fleet on October 5, 2026, and the new layout puts World Traveller Plus at 84 seats. That is the largest premium economy cabin on any A380 operating anywhere. The refurbished configuration runs 12 First, 106 Club Suites, 84 World Traveller Plus and 215 World Traveller for a total of 417 seats, down from 469 in the current layout. Economy absorbs the entire reduction, falling by 88 seats, while premium economy grows by more than half.

The proportions are what make the aircraft unusual. Head for Points calculated that the new layout gives British Airways 202 premium seats, roughly 48% of the aircraft, which is the highest premium ratio of any A380 operator by a wide margin. Emirates and Qantas are the next closest at around 30%, and both carry at least 56 fewer premium seats than the refurbished BA aircraft will. Since the A380 is the largest passenger aircraft ever built, that also gives British Airways the highest absolute number of premium seats on any single aircraft in service.

The timing matters for anyone trying to book. The first refurbished aircraft is expected in service within months, with the program running into 2027 and 2028, which means the 84-seat cabin will exist alongside the older 55-seat World Traveller Plus layout across the fleet for an extended period. British Airways operates 12 A380s to a small number of destinations from London Heathrow Airport (LHR), so the chance of drawing a refurbished aircraft on any given booking depends on how far along the program the fleet has progressed.

Emirates won the Skytrax award for the best premium economy seat in 2026, finishing ahead of Cathay Pacific and EVA Air, and The Points Guy named it Best International Premium Economy for a second consecutive year. AirlineRatings.com's passenger-voted Flyers' Choice Awards also named Emirates the preferred premium economy. The airline ranks second overall for premium economy with Skytrax and first for both the seats and the catering.

The A380 cabin seats 56 passengers within a four-class layout of 14 first, 76 business, 56 premium economy and 341 economy, giving the aircraft 146 premium seats and a premium ratio of around 30%. The seats are leather with adjustable headrests, calf rests, and footrests, and Emirates serves multicourse meals on china tableware with stainless steel cutlery, which is closer to a business class service pattern than most premium economy products attempt.

Scale is the other factor. Emirates operates roughly half the world's A380 fleet, with 116 aircraft, about 100 of which are active, and premium economy is arriving through the carrier's retrofit program rather than on new deliveries. The cabin joined Dubai to Milan on A380 services from November 10, 2025, alongside New York routes, and Emirates confirmed plans to offer premium economy on 68 routes by March 2026. Given the size of the fleet receiving it, a passenger searching for premium economy on a superjumbo will find more Emirates departures than from any other operator.

The cost of the world's longest A380 premium economy fares typically range within the $2,000-2,500, although this varies by discounts and season.

Qantas carries 60 premium economy seats on its A380s within a four-class layout of 14 first, 70 business, 60 premium economy and 341 economy, totaling 485 seats. That gives the aircraft 144 premium seats and a premium ratio of around 30%, effectively matching Emirates. The seats offer more width and legroom than economy, with adjustable headrests, footrests, storage and charging ports, and the fare includes priority check-in, priority boarding, upgraded meals, noise-canceling headphones and an amenity kit. Qantas operates 10 A380s, with around eight active, on routes from Sydney and Melbourne to London, Los Angeles, Dallas and Singapore.

Lufthansa fits 52 premium economy seats into a 509-seat A380 alongside 8 first, 78 business and 371 economy. The carrier operates eight aircraft and has committed to the type while retiring other quadjets, with summer 2026 routes including Los Angeles, Boston, Denver, Washington and Delhi, plus a new Munich to Mumbai service from July 2026. Lufthansa's premium economy ranks seventh overall in Skytrax's 2026 assessment and fifth for the seat specifically, though its highest-rated version is the Allegris cabin on the A350 and 787-9 rather than the A380.

Singapore Airlines has the smallest premium economy cabin on the type at 44 seats, in a 471-seat aircraft carrying 6 suites, 78 business and 343 economy. That is a premium ratio of around 27%, the lowest of the three. The product itself rates highly, placing third overall with Skytrax for premium economy and fourth for the seat, with calf and footrests, personal storage, charging ports and large entertainment screens. Premium economy passengers also get the Book the Cook service, allowing a main course to be reserved before departure, alongside priority check-in, boarding and baggage handling. The carrier operates 12 A380s across eight major routes from Changi for summer 2026.

All Nippon Airways carries 73 premium economy seats on its A380s, which ANA's own fleet material confirms in a 520-seat layout of 8 first, 56 business, 73 premium economy and 383 economy. That makes it the second-largest premium economy cabin on any A380, and Simple Flying reported it as the largest premium economy cabin across ANA's entire fleet. The seats sit at the rear of the upper deck behind first and business, arranged 2-3-2.

The aircraft itself is unlike any other A380 operation. ANA has three, each painted in a different sea turtle livery, and every scheduled A380 flight operates between Narita and Daniel K. Inouye International Airport (HNL) in Honolulu. No other operator confines the type to a single route. The aircraft entered service in May 2019 and continues to fly Narita to Honolulu year-round, with ANA adjusting frequencies seasonally. The third airframe only entered commercial service in late 2023 after an extended period in storage during the pandemic.

The configuration reflects who the aircraft carries. ANA built it around the Hawaii leisure market, and the cabin mix shows it, with 73 premium economy seats against 56 in business. The economy deck includes ANA COUCHii, where groups of three or four seats convert into a sofa-style space with raisable leg rests for an additional fee, which Airport Spotting described as aimed at families traveling with young children. For a passenger wanting premium economy on an A380, ANA offers one of the largest cabins available and the only route where you can find it.

A pro-Russian Serbian politician has allegedly caused a jet to return to Belgrade after dodging security

Four airlines fly the A380 with no premium economy cabin. Qatar Airways configures its aircraft with 8 first, 48 business and 461 economy seats across 517 total, giving it 56 premium seats and a premium ratio of around 11%, the lowest of any A380 operator by a substantial margin. Simple Flying excluded Qatar entirely from an earlier comparison of premium economy fares on the world's longest A380 routes, noting the airline does not offer the cabin on the type.

Etihad, Korean Air and Asiana complete the group. Etihad operates nine A380s and Asiana six, with Asiana running a three-class layout that includes 104 upper deck economy seats arranged 2-4-2. Those Asiana aircraft are also on borrowed time. Simple Flying reported that following the Korean Air merger, both carriers were expected to retire the type as part of fleet optimization.

The division is not a straightforward premium versus budget split. Qatar Airways has one of the most highly regarded business class products in the industry in Qsuite, yet its A380 carries the smallest proportion of premium seating of any operator of the type. A passenger wanting more than economy on a Qatar A380 has to buy business outright, with no intermediate option. Across the roughly 10 airlines still operating the A380, six offer premium economy and four do not, meaning the cabin is available on most superjumbo flights but not all of them.

25 Years Later, Buffy's Riskiest Episode Is Still a Brilliant Masterpiece That Changed the Show Forever

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

On Nov. 6, 2001, Buffy the Vampire Slayer made TV history. Again. The Emmy-winning series was already deep into its seven-season run when creator Joss Whedon came up with perhaps his most ambitious idea yet: a musical episode. The result was Buffy season 6 episode 7, "Once More, With Feeling," which remains a high point for the show and a frequent contender for best episode ever among diehard fans.

“Once More, With Feeling” isn't just a great musical (although it is) or a great episode of Buffy (it's also that). The brilliance of this 50-minute spectacle is how it combines the two, using a demon's curse that forces everyone in the town of Sunnydale to sing and dance as if they were in a Broadway play to push the show's overarching plot forward in numerous ways.

The best example of this may be the relationship between Buffy (Sarah Michelle Gellar) and Spike (James Marsters). The two had already been engaging in a toxic will-they-won't-they relationship for one season, and Spike takes the opportunity to get some big emotions off his chest while channeling Billy Idol in one of the musical’s best songs. At the end of the episode, when the curse is lifted but the revealed emotions still remain, Buffy and Spike share their first real kiss. Spoiler alert: their relationship does not go well.

But we're not here to relitigate "Spuffy." We're here to celebrate "Once More, With Feeling." To do so, I called up Marsters to recount the tale behind Buffy's iconic musical, from the terrifying moment he first found out about it to the episode's legacy 25 years later. Below, Polygon presents the behind-the-scenes story of "Once More, With Feeling" in the words of its breakout star.

You might think Joss Whedon would gather his cast to tell them they were about to make a musical. According to Marsters, he shared the news in a more abrupt way.

James Marsters: Instead of delivering the new script for the next episode, we were given a cassette tape with Joss singing the songs of the show and plunking them out on the piano. And whereas Joss is an enormously gifted writer and director and producer, as a singer he wasn't quite up to the material. As a piano player, he's fine, but it didn't sound like it was gonna work. It just sounded cheesy. I'm a punk rocker, and I was like, “What the F is this?”

I remember ripping the cassette tape out of the deck in my trailer and walking out into the bright sunshine and squinting and holding up this cassette tape, and everybody else was doing the same thing, opening their doors and just going, “What the fuck? Did you just listen to this? What's going on here?” It just set off this deep panic as we discovered that we were going to be asked to suddenly do a musical.

While a few of the cast members, including Marsters and Anthony Head (Giles) had professional singing experience, most did not.

Tony Head and I were both comfortable singing in public. I'd been singing in bars since I was 13. I was in a band, which I'm still in now, called Ghost of the Robot. Tony played Frank-N-Furter on the West End in Rocky Horror Picture Show. But the rest of the cast had a really good point, which is like, “I'm not a singer. You hired me to be a one-camera dramatic slash comedic actor, and that's what I do. And now you're gonna have me go out in front of tens of millions of people, maybe across the world, maybe a hundred million people, and do something that I'm not a professional at. You're gonna ruin my career.” That's actually not a bad point to make. I think Sarah asked to juggle chainsaws rather than have to sing — like that would be safer for her career.

So there's all this gnashing of teeth. And at some point, I think we all realized there was no way we were getting out of this. It was gonna happen no matter what we felt about it, which is a good thing. You don't wanna be asking actors all the time, “Are you comfortable doing this?” You don't want a TV show where the actors are in control. You want the producers and the writers to be in control if you want a good show.

Once the cast accepted their fate, they got to work and even brought in personal trainers to develop the skills they needed. However, it still took seeing an early cut of one sequence from the musical to win most of them over.

The reason why I'm so proud of us as a company, and why it is one of my favorite episodes, is that even though we were sure that we were going to go down in flames and all of our careers were over, even though we were sure that this show was going to get canceled very quickly after we tried this, we decided to go down swinging. We decided to try our best anyway, in the face of certain doom. People started hiring their own vocal coaches and their own dance instructors on their own dime. All these people were showing up to set. People were rehearsing and preparing in the middle of filming the previous episodes.

They knew we were freaking out. So the first thing they filmed was the Xander-Anya sequence, the dance and the song. They did a rough cut of that and showed it to us. And it was wonderful. It was amazing. We realized this thing actually was gonna be really good.

Marsters initially struggled to find a way to perform his big song, "Rest in Peace," but ultimately figured it out by taking some inspiration from an unlikely rock and roll icon. He also praises the song for expressing Spike's emotions at that point in the series in a way the character never would have been able to through normal written dialogue.

I didn't really know how to sing that song. So I started playing it on guitar, and I hit something in the way I was voicing the chords. It started working for me and I found my way in. I brought my guitar up to the production offices and I was like, “I found it! This is how we gotta do it,” and I started playing. The producers go, “That's amazing, James. That would be like if Nirvana did the song.” I was like, “Exactly!” And they said, “No, this is not Nirvana. This is like a Broadway rock song. So it's got to fit in. It is different from the other songs, but it's got to fit in that show.” And I was like, “F you guys. I'm gonna sing it that way anyway. Even if you're not gonna do the instrumentation, I'm gonna keep what I found.”

There's a lot of frustration pent up in Spike. Buffy has been kind of like, “Come here, get away from me. Come here, get away from me.” I have experienced that as a man, and I'm sure women experience the same thing. So I was like: Thank God I get to express it. How wonderful would it be to just break out in a song and say, "Babe, either take me or don't. You're just torturing me right now."

That's one of the many things that was genius about the idea. I heard long ago that for the best musicals, there comes a point in the dramatic story where the character wants to express something that words won't express. They need another tool to get there so they can tell the person what's really up. And song and dance are the only way to do it.

There's this lift in the story, in the character, in the emotion that happens when you break out into song. Joss knew that and he used that to get his characters to say things that they normally never would tell each other — secrets that people keep hidden from each other — and the chaos that can happen if that stuff is let out all in one day.

So why does this one episode hold up so well? For Marsters, it's not just because the songs are great and the plot is exciting, it's because it speaks to the inspiring theme at the heart of Buffy the Vampire Slayer.

The musical episode is most people's favorite episode of the show, and I think that's because it is frankly one of the best episodes of television that I'm aware of. It risked more. There was more work put into it. There was more creativity put into that episode than almost any other episode that I have ever seen. And I think the risk is really part of it.

The theme of Buffy is: Don't give up. It's hard to be a human being. It hurts to be here. The world is very imperfect, but don't give up on yourself. Don't give up on the world. Find something to fight for. What you decide to fight for is up to you. It might be taking care of your mom. It might be marching in the streets. But find the thing that's important to you and do it and don't give up on it. For one episode, we spoiled Hollywood actors lived up to the theme of the show. And then, luckily and wonderfully, it didn't suck.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

This flew so Doctor Horrible could soar and ask these years later it’s still the best thing anyone has ever self-published on the internet.
